[trialAccount t18360404-969]
969. PHILIP PARR and JOSEPH SNELL were indicted for a robbery on William Clerk, on the 4th of March, putting him in fear and taking from his person, and against his will, 1 watch, value 2 l.; 4 seals, value 2 l.; and 1 watch-chain, value 6 d.; his property.
WILLIAM CLARK . I am a cooper's labourer, and live in Easton-street. On Friday evening, the 4th of March, about half-past eight o'clock, I was coming from home, and going to Laystall-street—coming up Mount Pleasant, I saw two young men scrummaging or fighting together; I could not well tell which—I went on one side—I passed them—Parr after me, and caught me round the waist, and threw me into the kennel—I did not know him before—he thew me down and doubled his knee into my belly—I lost my senses then for a bit, and laid flat on my back, with my heard being bruised against the paved stones—when I recovered, I got on my hands and knees—I found my watch lying on the pavement by the side of me—the buttons were all torn off my waistcoat, which was torn open; and I found my watch-fob pulled right out of my trowsers—I got up, and put my watch into my side-pocket, and buttoned my coat—I followed the prisoner Parr into Pool's-buildings—he returned from Pool's-buildings into Laystall-street, when I met a policeman, and gave him in charge—the policeman took him into a wine-vault to search him, and asked me for the watch—I delivered it to him—I lost the seals and things, besides the watch—they were all lying by my side—I picked them all off the pavement.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did Parr appear to be very drunk? A. He put his hands round me threw me down, doubled his
knee into me, and made me black all round—I did not know that the prisoners belonged to the parish workhouse—I cannot say whether my head was on the ground—I cannot say about what I was very queer in my head, and did not walk steady and comfortably, althouogh I would follow him—I been ill before—I do not think I laid on the ground more than five minutes—I had taken nothing to drink that day—I was under the doctor's hands—I did not see any body take my watch—it laid about a foot from me I was thrown down by main force—I cannot say whether Parr was drunk—I never saw him before, but the two men were struggling together as if drunk, or going to fight—did not lose ant thing—I do not think Parr and drunk—when the policeman took him he pretended to be drunk, but at the station-house he spoke as well as I could speak—he might have had a drop of drink, but he came to his recollection, talking to the gentleman there; but as for Snell, I could not swear he is the man who was with him—I saw two togeth—I had 3 d. in my hand, and that I kept in my hand—I never loosened my money—my hand was clenched—I was merely going out to get some saveloys for supper.
ROBERT MATTHEWS . I am a steel pen maker, and five in Baynes-row, Clerkenwell. On the 4th of March, I was coming down Baynes-row, towards Mount Pleasant, about nine o'clock—I saw the prisoner insulting every person they met—they stopped a seafaring man, and he pushed them away from him, and passed on—directly after that I met the prosecutor coming along Mount Pleasant—the two prisoners pushed him about ten yards—the prisoner Parr turned round, went up to the prosecutor, took him round the waist, threw him on the ground, and knelt on him, and drew his watch from his fob—when he saw me he let the watch fall on the pavement—he got up and staggered away, pretending to be intoxicated, and went round into Pool's-buildings—I brought him out of there, down to the prosecutor, who was standing in Mount Pleasant—there being no policeman present, we let him go, and followed him till was we went a policeman in Laystall-street, and then gave him into custody—the prisoner Snell was ten yards off at the time Parr knocked the prosecutor down—Snell was staggering about at the time—he went to the workhouse, and we took him into custody at the workhouse, in bed, about an hour afterwards—when Parr was waiting with the prosecutor, after he had thrown him down, Snell called to him to come on.
Cross-examined. Q. Is Baynes's-row, the address you gave at the station-house? A. It is the same address—it was not Red Lion-street—is did not take a minute for Parr to throw the prosecutor down and draw his watch—it might be two minutes after that I took Parr—I followed him round directly and took him—I should think in two minutes the outside of the time—it might be three or four minutes—when I came back I found the prosecutor standing up in Mount Pleasant—I consider Parr war sober—he had been drinking—Mount Pleasant is a good thoroughfare—there were they very few people going by at the time—I saw nobody but our two selves at the time it happened—there was no other person passing except the prosecutor, and he was knocked down—I was close to the prosecutor when Parr threw him down—I heard his head fall against my foot—I should think Parr might have seen me—we did not lose sight of him when we let him gohe walked up Laystall-street, round the corner—I ascertained that both the prisoners belonged to the work-house—Pool did not run away—the watch was not broken—the prosecutor took his watch up to
look if it was broken—Parr did not make off with the watch at all—he dropped it by the side of him.
Q. Do not you think it very difficult to distinguish whether this was a drunken attempt to molest the prosecutor or to rob him? A. I had seen them insult several people before—I said to Parr, "You call yourself drunk?"—he said, "No, I am sober"—I said, "You have been drinking."
FREDERICK NOYES . I live in Clerk's-place, Bagnigge-wells-road. I was with Matthews, and saw the prisoners insulting every person they met—I saw Parr throw the prosecutor down and draw his watch from his pocket—I and Matthews stepped up at the time, and he let go of the watch—I saw him drop it—we went up to the prosecutor, and saw him pick the watch up from the pavement—we followed Parr, and gave him in charge of a policeman—Snell was about ten yards distant at the time, staggering about.
Cross-examined. Q. This taking hold of the prosecutor, taking his watch, and throwing him down, must have taken a great while, I suppose? A. Not a great while—I suppose it took half an hour.
HENRY JOHNSON . I am a policeman. I was going home from this Court on the 4th of March—I saw Parr pass me, and the prosecutor, and two witnesses—about a minute afterwards they gave him in my charge—I took Snell at the work-house about an hour afterwards—the prisoners were in liquor, but not so drunk as not to know what they were doing.
Cross-examined. Q. When you found the prisoner had got nothing, but that the prosecutor had got the watch, you might have taken the man before a Magistrate, and had them committed as rogues and vagrants, might you not? A. Certainly—there was no bruise on the watch—Parr told me he belonged to the workhouse, and said where we could find Snell.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Justice Patteson.
[trialAccount t18360404-970]
970. GEORGE WOOD was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of David Wheway, about the hour of nine o'clock in the night of the 18th of February, at St. Matthew, Bethmal-green, with intent to steal, and stealing there in 1 watch, value 2 l. 10 s.; 1 watch-key, value 6 d.; 1 watch ribbon, value 2 d.; 1 purse, value 2 d.; and 20 shillings, his goods and monies.
DAVID WHEWAY . I am a silk-weaver, and live at No. 28, New Nicholl-street, St. Matthew, Bethnal-green. The prisoner was my apprentice—his time was up about twelve moths ago—he remained with me after that, till within the last month—about the 4th of February—he came back on the 18th of February, I believe—that is the day I missed my watch and silver—he was not in my employ at that time—he had got a fresh place a week previous to that—he remained at my house about half an hour in the kitchen—I have a workshop on the third floor—it is under the same roof as the rest of the house—he went away about eleven o'clock in the morning—I occupy the whole house—I work in the workshop myself—I came down between nine or ten o'clock at night, from the workshop—I left my purse, with about 20 s. in silver, in one of the drawers of a chest in the workshop, and my watch in one of the drawers—I am quite sure the workshop door was latched—I was the last person in it—I did not go out of
the house at all after that—I cannot tell whether the outer door was on the latch or not; but when I came down stairs, about half-past nine o'clock, it was on the latch, but very likely my wife or the boys may have gone out afterwards—I went to bed about ten o'clock—I and my wife sleep in the kitchen, and my two apprentices sleep in the workshop to protect it—they went to bed before me, and my wife also—I am certain the street door was shut when I went to bed—is was on the latch, and bolted inside, and the windows were fast—about half-past nine o'clock in the evening we were sitting by the fire—I had scarcely sat down before I heard the street door go-that was the moment I came down in the evening—and, it appears to me, that at that time the robbery was done—the boys and come down to have their supper—we were all down in the kitchen, and I heard the street-door alarm too—said, "The street-door is gone too, and I have heard nobody go up or down"—the boy opened the door, with a candle, and said, "Master, here is nobody"—I said, "Very well, shut the door"—the street-door was latched at the time I came down form the workshop, but not bolted, and very probably my wife might have gone out on an errand, or the boys had gone out for beer, and left it unbolted—I cannot tell whether it was on the latch or not just before I heard it alarm too—the workshop-door was latched when I left it—I was the last person who came out of it—I did not go up to it again that night—I went up next morning, and perceived my silver was gone—I opened the drawer, and the watch was gone—I came down, and told my wife—I then went to the prisoner's lodging, and said to him, "George, I was robbed last night of about 1 l. worth of silver and my watch, do you know any thing of it?"—he said, No, master, I do not"—he said, "It is very strange; you must know something about it, for no other person could have access to my shop, only you that were used to it"—he said, "I was going to come to your house this morning, about some work"—and he came in about an hour to my house—I said again, "You must know something of it, for nobody but you knew how to come to my house"—he then left—this was on Friday—on the Saturday it got reported in the neighbourhood, and a neighbour gave me information—I went to him again on the Monday morning, and charged him with it again—he denied it, and I took him to the station-house—when I got against the station-house, I said, "Don't left me go in here; it will be the worst day's work you ever had; give me the watch, and you may keep the silver, if you like"—he said he knew nothing about it—I said, "Well, if you have not got it in your possession, send it by dinner-time"—I did not like to take him inside the house—on the 10th, one of my workmen brought me a duplicate—he is not here—the watch was worth 50 s.—I lost about 20 s., in money.
THOMAS LORD BEESTON . I am foreman to Mr. Whiskard, a pawnbroker. I produce a watch which I took in pawn from the prisoner on the 22nd of February—I am certain of him—he gave his name "James Wood."
HENRY BARKER . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner—I have a duplicate, which the prosecutor gave me—I told the prisoner what I took him for—he said he was very sorry for it—I cautioned him not to say any more.
NATHANIEL BENFIELD . I am an apprentice to the prosecutor. I only know that the prisoner was at the house on the day of the robbery.
DAVID WHEWAY re-examined. This is the duplicate I received from my workman, and this is my watch.
THOMAS LORD BEESTON re-examined. This is the duplicate I gave for
the watch—I have the fellow one—I gave it to the person who pawned it—I am certain that was prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. I was in great distress—he promised me work, and did not perform his word, and I was out of work—I had nothing to apply to—I went two or three days to ask him to give me work, and he said "No."
DAVID WHEWAY re-examined. I did not promise him work—he did not leave me for any misconduct—I recommended him to another place—his character was very good with me—I believe he was in distress—I would take him into my employ again.
GUILTY. of stealing only.—Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor and Jury. — Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Justice Gaselee.
[trialAccount t18360404-971]
971. WILLIAM PRESCOTT was indicted , for that he, on the 31st day of March, in and upon Henry Ellerby Turner, unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously, did make an assault, and then and there unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously did wound him in and upon the forehead, with intent feloniously, wilfully, and of his malice aforethought, to kill and murder him.—2nd COUNT; stating it to be to disfigure and disable him.—3rd COUNT. stating it to be to do him some grievous bodily harm.
HENRY ELLERBY TURNER . I am a paper-bag maker, and live in Sherwood-place, Bethnal-green. I have known the prisoner eighteen months—we have been very good friends—we have not fallen out till within the last four of five weeks, but for four or five months we have been at variance in his sort of way—I don't know why. On the 31st of March of came home from Clapton, between twelve and one o'clock at night—I was knocking at my door—the prisoner came out—he lives right opposite me, and he called me a hopping b—several times—I asked him why he did so—he struck me with some weapon—I thought it was a hammer, but I don't know—it took my senses away completely—he struck me in the forehead, over the left eye—I only just recollect those words as I fell—it took my senses away for a minute or two, I suppose—I lost them altogether—my forehead bled violently—I was covered with blood—my wife helped to dress my wound, as I have heard since—I went to the hospital, but I would not stop, as I wanted to get home to my wife and family—I went to bed directly after it was done—I found my self in bed—I don't recollect going to bed at all—I was called up by the police, when they took the prisoner which was almost directly, I believe, but I can't say—and I went to the station-house, and from thence to the hospital—they locked the prisoner up at the station-house—I was about half an hour in the hospital—they would not dress the wound, because it had swollen so—I went to the hospital next morning—they never saw the place at all, for the surgeon was not come when I went there, and I would not stop—I have been confined at home with it, until last Monday—from Thursday to Monday—I just took a walk out, but no distance—I don't know how the prisoner came to do this, but he has been at variance about six weeks—I cannot say what about, but I have asked him for a soldering-iron, or copper bit, which he has got of mine.
EDMUND M'CARTHY . I am a policeman. On the day in question I heard a scream and call for the police—I went towards Sherwood-place, where the prosecutor lives—I saw the prosecutor opposite his own door—he was all over blood—I knocked at the prisoner's door, but they would not open it—he lives opposite to the prosecutor—I do not know whether he was there when I knocked, for a few minutes after we went
into the house, and he was not in the house—I saw him about half an hour after—the sergeant and I (came round again and heard) the prisoner rushing out of the back door of his own house—he rushed into the door of the next house, and we took him up-stairs in the bed-room—we took him to the station-house—I told him he had nearly killed the man—he made me no answer—George Teakle was with me—I did not find any instrument with which the prosecutor had been struck.
GEORGE TEAKLE . I was with M'Carthy when I took the prisoner—he told him what he took him for—he made no answer—on the way to the station-house, the prosecutor said he had struck him with a hammer—the prisoner heard it—he made no answer—I returned back to the prisoner's house, and found this hammer in the cupboard by the side of the fire—the prisoner did not see it—he said nothing till he was before the magistrate.
Prisoner's Defence. This prosecutor came home about twelve o'clock—he came to my door, and knocked as if he was going to burst the door in, and kicked it, calling me and my wife all the gravy-eyed b—he could think of—for nearly half an hour that lasted; and at last I opened the door, and asked him what he meant by calling me and my wife such names—he came into my house when I opened the door, and attempted to strike me two or three times, but I escaped from him; and in the house he laid hold of me by the collar, and attempted to strike me on the back, just as I was by the fire-place—I stooped down by the fire-place, and got a little bit of a poker—I told him, if he did not let go of me, and get out of my house, that I should strike him—he would not leave go of me, but dragged me nearly to the door, and told me to come out—I said I would not and if he did not leave go of me I would hit him with the bit of poker I had in my hand—that is all.
SARAH RUTTER . I am a neighbour of the prisoner's, and live nearly opposite him. I heard the prosecutor come home, about twenty minutes before one o'clock in the morning—apparently by his voice he was tipsy—his next door neighbour was with him—he made a noise and awoke us—the next door neighbour's stairs join mine and I heard him go up-stairs—it was not a minute after that I heard the prosecutor cross the road to the prisoner's door—he commended knocking violently—he said, "Come out, you b—I have just come home in good trim for you"—I heard the prisoner's door open and shut quickly again—I heard the prosecutor say, "You cowardly b—, you have cut my head with a hammer"—the prosecutor's wife by that time came down, and said to the prisoner, "Come out, you coward, and stand before me"—she said she would have his life—the prosecutor and his wife then cried "Police," and an Irish policeman, (I know by his tongue,) came up—he asked the policeman to take charge of the prisoner, and he said he could not, as he did not see the blow—the prosecutor said, "What! can't you take charge of him? "then I am to be murdered because you did not see it done?"—he said, "Then I'm b—if you do your duty"—by that time the policeman and the prosecutor went away—the wife desired him to go to a doctor and get the wound dressed—I think it was about twenty minutes or a quarter to two o'clock when I heard them come back again—I heard nothing then till I saw the prisoner brought thought his next door neighbour's house—that is all know—I believe the prisoner struck the prosecutor, but in his own defence—he never went out of his own door by what I saw—I saw the light shine through my binds, and the door shut again directly.
JURY. Q. Could you see this clearly? A. I did not see—I only
heard—my husband would not allow me to get out of bed—he said we were so annoyed by the prosecutor—I knew the prosecutor's voice very well—we are very much annoyed by his coming home at night late.
EDWARD M'CARTHY re-examined. When I took the prisoner, he was up-stairs in a bed-room, along with a lot of children, in the adjoining house—I did not notice that he had received any blows at all.
GEORGE TEAKLE re-examined. The prisoner had no apprearance of any blows at all—there was never a word said about this before.
SARAH RUTTER re-examined. I do not think the prisoner got away blows; for, from what I could hear, he opened the door, gave the prosecutor a knock, and shut the door again immediately.
SAMUEL RUTTER . I am the last witness's husband, and am a painter and glazier. I was at home in bed and asleep, and heard my next door neighbour's wife, Mrs. Emmet, I believe—she awoke me—I then heard the prosecutor say, "I am come home in right trim; come out, you b—and I am ready for you"—then I heard a violent knocking at the door—then the door was shut and opened again—I think then the prosecutor said the prisoner had struck him with a hammer—he repeated that over and over twenty times at least—soon after I heard his wife come out—there was a violent knocking at the door, and then the police was called—the prosecutor's wife went and knocked at the prisoner's door, and told him to come out, that she would have his life, and a great deal of that—she was ready for him—they then broke the shutters open at the next door neighbour's, and took the prisoner out of their house—when he came out he had no shoes on, and he asked the policeman to let him go in and put his shoes on, but they would not let him—then the policeman took him away—he said his shoes were under the children's bed—I believe this is one of the policemen—I was in bed all the time—I did not see any thing—I did not get out of bed, for there had been disturbance before—I said it was a shame we should be disturbed by these people—I think the prosecutor did very wrong—he had no business at the prisoner's door.
JURY. Q. Being in bed, could you tell whether it was the prisoner at the prosecutor's door, or the prosecutor at the prisoner's door? A. I could swear to that man's voice from a thousand—the prisoner lives nearly opposite, and the prosecutor lives next door to me—the street in seven or eight yards wide—I thought the prosecutor was in liquor by his tongue that night—the prisoner is not a quarrelsome character. I think, when sober.
NOT GUILTY .
Second Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18360404-972]
972. MARY DAVIES was indicted for feloniously braking and entering the dwelling-house of James Sweet, on the 28th of March, at St. Luke, Middlesex, and stealing there in 2 shawls, value 9 s.; 1 frock, value 2 s.; 2 aprons, value 1 s.; 4 petticoats, value 3 s.; 2 pinafores, value 6 d.; 12 caps, value 2 s.; and 2 shirts, value 2.; his property.—2nd COUNT. stating it to be the dwelling-house of Charlotte Sweet.
CHARLOTTE SWEET . I am the wife of James Sweet, a private in the 89th Regiment of Foot Guards, I live in Playhouse-yard, Whitecross-street, in the parish of St. Luke-the prisoner came there, about the 25th of March, and took a furnished room—it was on a Friday—she was to pay 3 s. 6 d. a week—I required a reference—She told me where I could get one, but I was confined at the time, and did not inquire after it—I saw her on the Tuesday in the house—she and Sarah
Smith came out at the street door, about the middle of the day—they had both something in their aprons—I went up to my bed-room, and found it in confusion—I missed from my drawers two shawls, two aprons, one frock, two pinafores, a bundle of caps two shirts, and four petticoat—I went to the station-house, got an officer, and gave the prisoner in charge—she had very little to say—she pretended to know nothing at all shout it—she was taken to the office, and there I found on her a flannel petticoat, which was taken from my room—I had locked the bed-room door in the morning, and had not opened it afterwards—it must have been opened by a key for I found it still locked, and I am confident the things were there in the morning—I told the prisoner the petticoat was mine, and insisted on her taking it off—she said Smith had given her the petticoat—Smith had no business in my house at all—the prisoner paid me 1 s. 6 d. when she came into the house—she was to pay 3 s. 6 d. a week.
JESSE TUSTIN ( police-constable G 41.) On Tuesday afternoon, the 29th of March, Mrs. Sweet came to the station-house—I accompanied her house, and after I was there some time the prisoner came in—I took her to the station-house—I tried the key of the prisoner's room door, and found it would open the door of Mrs. Sweet's room—at the office Mrs. Sweet claimed a petticoat which the prisoner had on-nothing else has been found.
CHARLOTTE SWEET re-examined. This is my petticoat—it is made of two different sorts of flannel—the salvages are at the bottom instead of being made up at the sleeves—it was in the box under my bedstead—I have found nothing else.
Q. How came you, seeing two persons coming down with an apron full of things, not to stop them?— A. I stood at my counter in the shop—I went up-stairs, unlocked my door, and found the things about—I had not been in my room since eight o'clock in the morning—they came down between one and two o'clock in the day—I did not know they had got my things then—I am confident nobody went up-stairs, or I must have seen them—I have two more lodgers—I missed nothing from the prisoner's room—she said her husband was a carman, but I find he is a cabman—my husband does not live in the house with me—I pay the rent, and I have occupied it six years.
Prisoner's Defence. I don't know any thing about it—Smith gave me the petticoat—I did not know but it was her own—she gave it to me, and told me to put it on, as it was cleaner than my own was, and I did so—we both went out to get some things for tea—I left her talking to some persons in Whitecross-street—I came back after going for the things for tea, and I could not find Smith again—I went home and went up stairs, and asked if she had been—they said nobody had been at all—I sat down to my tea, and two policemen came in and said I had robbed Mrs. Sweet—Mrs. Sweet found the key of my door would open her's—she said she knew it would open her door, bur she had not time to get another—she keeps a house for girls like myself, and takes in things of all descriptions, and pledges—she has taken things from me—she charges 3 d., for 1 s.—if I go the same day to get things she charges 15 d.—girls have pawned pattens and different things of hers—if you pawn any thing at her place for 1 d., she charges you for it—if the house was searched, there are tickets and parcels folded up, and how much people have written on them—and when they fetch them away they have to pay so much interest.
CHARLOTTE SWEET re-examined. I did not know the key would fit my
door, or I should not have let her have it—I never took any thing from the prisoner—I never knew her before—I keep a broker's shop, and sell pails and things—I never advance money one things—my stock consists of tins, pails, brooms, clothes, beds, and wearing apparel—I write on things what I give for them, and put them at the door for sale—I don't keep any thing in my house that may be sold to the same people again—I do not let people have things back again.
JESSE TUSTIN re-examined. The prosecutor keeps a shop in the general line—she sells all kinds of apparel, shoes, clothes, tubs, and pails—things are not pawned there, to my knowledge—there was no duplicate found on the prisoner—inquiry has been made after Smith, but we cannot find her—Smith's mother said if her daughter was taken she would be sure to be transported this time—Smith is well known to us as a person of bad character.
GUILTY .* Aged 15.— Transported for Seven Years.

The same evidence was given in this case as on the prisoner's trial on the 7th day, when he was convicted of feloniously receiving the above articles.

[trialAccount t18360404-974]
974. GEORGE NOWLAN was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of March, 1 jacket value 2 s., the goods of John Savage and that he had been before convicted of felony.
WILLIAM SAVAGE . I am nephew and assistant to John Savage, a pawnbroker, in Whitechapel-road. On the night of the 24th of March I saw the prisoner near the door of the shop, pulling at some jackets which hung at the door-post—I called my uncle out—I had seen him before that, kick some boots which were placed at the door—I have since seen a jacket which hung at the door in the prisoner's possession.
JOHN SAVAGE . I am the last witness's uncle. About seven o'clock at night he called my attention to the jackets—I missed one—a policeman came, and I told him what I had lost—I afterwards went to Aaron's a pawnbroker, in Whitechapel-road, and saw the prisoner there—a jacket was produce to me, which was the one I had lost—that was near eight o'clock—I gave the prisoner in charge, and the policeman kept the jacket.
JOHN AARON . I am a pawnbroker, and live in Whitechapel-road. On the 24th of March, about eight o'clock, the prisoner came to my shop and offered the jacket to pawn, which Mr. Savage afterwards claimed—I asked him where he got it—he sad a person outside had given it him to pledge—I asked him if he knew anything about the person—he said, "No."
Prisoner. He did not ask me where I got it—I put it on the counter, and asked 3 s. on it from the shopman—it was taken away from me, and I saw no more of it till it was brought to the station-house, and there he said I was in the shop—the inspector asked how I came by it, and I said a man outside asked me to pawn it, but he never asked me about it in the shop. Witness. I did ask him about it in Mr. Savage's presence, I believe—I am positive I put the question to him when he produced the jacket—I
was not at the station-house at all—I had been told something was wrong, and was on the look-out in case it should be offered to pawn.
Prisoner. I said nothing in the shop about a man sending me to pawn it. Witness. I am positive he did.
EDWIN HELL . I am a policeman. I received the prisoner in charge, and took him to the station-house—the prisoner said in the shop, "I know nothing about the jacket, only a man outside sent me to pawn it"—I know nothing of him, he is a stranger to me.
Prisoner. Nothing was said to me about it till I got to Lambeth-street. Witness. It passed in the shop.
JOHN SAVAGE re-examined. This is the jacket I lost that evening—it has a private mark on the sleeve, part of which is torn out—Aaron told me, in the prisoner's presence, that he had asked the prisoner where he got it, and he said of a man outside.
WILLIAM LOWE ( police-constable H 73.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—I was present at the prisoner's trial—he is the man mentioned in the certificate—( read.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was that evening passing by a public-house, the sign of the two Bells, by the church—a man came from towards Brick-lane, and said, "Young man, are you willing to take a jacket in to pawn, and I will give you the price of a pot of beer?"—he seemed in liquor—I said, "Can't you take it yourself?"—he said, "I have a person I do not wish to leave; will you take it to pawn, and ask 3 s. on it?"—the shopman took it to the gas-light, and I never saw it afterwards—is it likely, if I stole it, I should take it only forty doors off to pawn it?
GUILTY .* Aged 26.— Transported Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18360404-975]
975. ELIZABETH KITE was indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 15th of March, of a certain evil-disposed person, 1 shirt, value 2 s., the goods of George Randall, well knowing it to have been stolen.
HARRIET RANDALL . I am the wife of George Randall, and live at Twickenham. On the 15th of March, a shirt and several articles were taken out of my garden in a wet state—the shirt was wet, just as I had wrong it out of the water—I went to a pawnbroker's at Isleworth, and found it there with the mark taken out, but I was satisfied it was the same, as I had made it myself—I received the dupliceates from Ann Taylor, to go to Isleworth to take it out—I met a man named William Ballard, when I went out on missing my property, and he asked me if I had lost any thing—he was about a quarter of a mile from my premises.
ANN TAYLOR . I keep a shop in Twickenham-common. The prisoner came to me on Thursday, the 17th of March, and said she had been dealing with another person, and they would not let her have any more things on account of her not having quite enough money to pay the last week's bill, and she was so distressed that she was obliged to pawn her husband's shirt—if I would let her have some money on the duplicate, she would leave it till Saturday night, and then would come and pay me, as her husband had no other shirt to were on the Sunday—she left me the duplicate of the shirt, and I let her have some bread out butter on it—she called the man she lives with her husband—his name is Messenger—next day the called with another woman, and said, "Don't let any body see that duplicate; if you do, my husband will make a great piece of work about my pawning his shirt"—I said, "No, I will not"—next morning the woman
came for more things—I refused to let her have them, and I saw no more of the prisoner—Mrs. Randall came to me to demand some linen of me, as she had heard I had some, but I had more—I said I bad the duplicate of a shirt—she said she would take it, and if she shirt was not hers she would return it—( looking at the duplicate) I cannot say whether that is it—I did not open it—I gave Mrs. Randall the some duplicate I received from the prisoner.
PHILIP HUMPHREYS . I am a pawnbroker, and live at Isleworth. I produce a shirt, pawned at my shop, on the 15th of March, by the prisoner, I am certain, in the name of Elizabeth Messenger, for 1 s. 6 d.—it was washed and ironed when I received it, about six o'clock in the evening—she said it was her husband's shirt—I live about a mile and a half from Mrs. Randall.
Prisoner. I never pawned any thing but my husband's clothes—I pawned every thing in my husband's name. Witness. She always pawned in Messenger's name—she did not pawn with me very often—not for a long time before.
JAMES BROMAGE . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner, and Ballard, whom I found at the prisoner's house, in her company—she door not go by the name of Messenger—I have known the prisoner some time—I told her I took her on a charge of felony; and when I was taking her into custody, Ballard came up, and stated that she should not go to the station-house, for he would take the charge on himself, for it was his property—that she had had the ticket from him—she did not say it was his property—he is no relation of her's—he is a bad character—the prisoner afterwards said, a young man, named Stuckey, offered it her for sale—she said she had no money, and he asked her to pawn it, which she did, for 1 s. 6 d., at Isleworth—I know Messenger—he lived with the prisoner.
Prisoner. I borrowed the money of my brother for the duplicate of the shirt—I pawned it myself, but I bought the duplicate afterwards of him—I did it out of a friendly act—Ballard and Stuckey told me it was their own—they stopped at my house ten minutes—as I could not buy it, they asked me to pawn it for them, which I did, and gave the money to him, and the ticket; and two or three days afterwards, Ballard came and asked me to buy the ticket—he said, "Your husband wanted a shirt"—I went to my brother, and borrowed the money, and gave him 1 s. 6 d. for it.
JAMES BROMAGE re-examined. The boys she names are bad characters—they are about eighteen or twenty years old—the Magistrate discharged Ballard—he said he found it in a ditch—I never knew the prisoner in custody.
NOT GUILTY .
NEW COURT,— Friday, April 8 th, 1836.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18360404-976]
976. JOHN TEMPLE was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 2 s., the goods of Frederick Bowman, from his person.
FREDERICK BOWMAN . I live in Great Alie-street, Goodman's-fields. I was in Leadenhall-street, on the 5th of April—I felt a had behind me—some one tapped me on the shoulder—my handkerchief was handed to me almost immediately, and Mr. Herbert had hold of the prisoner's collar—it was the handkerchief I had in my pocket just before—this is it.
THOMAS HERBERT . I was in Leadenhall-street, and saw the prisoner
put his hand to the prosecutor's pocket, take the handkerchief out, and put it into his coat—I seized him when he prosecutor seized him—he threw the handkerchief behind me.
Prisoner. It is false—my hand was down by my side. witness. I was in my employer's shop at the time they passed, and I saw him take it out of the gentleman's pocket—I seized him before he threw it from him—he then threw it down—my shopmate took it up, and gave it to the gentlemen.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18360404-977]
977. MARGARET HUMPHREYS was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of February, 1 locket, value 20 s.; 1 printed book, value 5 s.; 4 knives, value 5 s.; 4 forks, value 5 s.; 1 shift, value 5 s.; and 1 petticoat, value 5 s.; the goods of George Frederick Allcock, her master.
GEORGE FREDERICK ALLCOCK . I live in Park-terrace, Brixton. The prisoner lived with me, as cook, for eight days previous to the 12th of February—we missed a shift the day after she came into the house—when she left, on the 12th, in the morning, we missed the other things stated in the indictment—it was last in a drawer, that was kept locked, in the bed-room—she had access to the bed-room, but the key of the drawer was in my wife's possession—the prisoner had keys in her possession which opened my drawers—these are all articles.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS Q. Do you know a woman of the name of Price? A. Yes, and another of the name of Shaw—they are not acquaintances of mine—they live in the neighbourhood—I never spoke to them before the transaction—the prisoner came from Wales I believe—she sent a letter by me to town—I do not remember my having a dispute with my wife about it—I never told her that my wife was amazed at her sending a letter by me, and making me a postman—I never heard my wife say so—she was not jealous about it—she was angry—I had never given a present to this girl—I swear that—not of any kind—I did not speak to her about my wife's anger till she left the house—I saw her at Mr. Shaw's house—I went to his after she had left my service.
Q. Did you tell her about your wife's anger? A. She did not tell me—she made a declaration in the presence of me and Mr. Shaw, but not to me.
Q. What brought you there at all? A. Within an hour after she left the house, she came to my counting-house, to stated that the party whom she had introduced into the house the day before, (who turned out to he Mr. Shaw,) that Mrs. Allcock, when Mr. Shaw went down there, had stated, that she was such an infamous bad character she could not keep her in the house, and she would give him anything to take her away—she came to say she should apply to the police-office for a warrant against my wife—I went round to Shaw immediately, to know what had been stated, and then this declaration was made—I afterwards went to Mr. Shaw, and saw him—he had not been in our service—he was sent for to our house by the prisoner, to see her—I heard that from Shaw, and the prisoner was present at the time—I stayed there about a quarter of an hour—I never saw the prisoner afterwards till she was in custody—I did not give her in charge—she was in charge on another felony—I swear that I missed the things three or four days after she left, and I found her in charge, between a fortnight and three weeks afterward—I did not give her in charge, because she was not to be found—I went to Mr. Shaw's for her—I did not got to her aunt's—I went to a Mr. Williams, in Hatton-garden—no flirtation
has taken place between us—I was married at St. Giles's, Camberwell, about fifteen years ago—I never taken any liberties with her—I have never sworn that my wife ever spoke to me about carrying that letter—it was given in the presence of my wife—she spoke to me about it—but did not remonstrate with me—she wanted to know who the letter was for, and in her presence, I said it was for her aunt—she begged the favour of me to give it to her aunt if she called at the counting-house—that was the whole of the conversation about the letter—she wanted to know who the letter was for—I said it was for this Mrs. Shaw, whom she represented to be her aunt—that was on the evening of the 17th, and she gave me the letter in the morning—I did not promise I would take this girl as cook in my town house, at sixteen guineas a-year—it is necessary for me to keep up an establishment in town—I had no communication with her—nobody was present when she was at my counting-house.
COURT. Q. Upon your solemn oath, did you ever, directly or indirectly, allow her to take any of this property? A. Upon my solemn oath I never did.
CATHERINE PRICE . I live at Cross Keys-square, Little Britain, and am the wife of Thomas Price. On the 9th of January, the prisoner came to lodge with me—she had this box with her at that time.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you know Mr. Allcock before? A. Never, Sir, no more than his being overseer of the parish—I had seen him once, and spoken to him—the prisoner came from Wales—she stated so—she appeared a stranger.
THOMAS ELLIS ( police-sergeant C 7.) I took these things from that box, which I found at No. 5, Harbour-street, Red Lion-square, on the 1st of March.
Cross-examined. Q. So that if this girl had any consciousness that these things were stolen, she had from the 12th of February to the 1st of March to dispose of them? A. Certainly she had.
MARY SHAW . I am the wife of William Shaw, of No, 3, Cross Keys-square. The prisoner lodged with me on the 9th of January—she was with me in the day time.
THOMAS ELLIS re-examined. Q. What house did you find the box at? A. At Mrs. Smith's—we could not get her to come forward, but the prisoner said it was her box.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was the box there the prisoner at the time? A. No; but she asked what I found in the box.
COURT. Q. Tell us that passed.? A. She said, "Are you the officer that searched my box?"—I said, "Yes asked me how much silver I found there—I told her 3 s.—she asked me to let her have 1 s. of it—I told her I could not do so at present—the parish constable was with me at the time—he said, "I will rend her a shilling—when you return that money stop that, and give it to me before the Magistrate"—I told that, and returned the 2 s., and gave him one.
JURY. Q. Where did you take her? A. In Leather-lane—on this charge—I believe this charge is in our police-sheet—Mrs. Price gave her in charge, on this charge; but she made another charge against her.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was it not on the charge of stealing from Mr. Allcock that she was given into your custody? A. Yes it was.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18360404-978]
978. GEORGE BAKER was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of March, 1 iron gate, value 20 s., the goods of Joseph Henry Roberts, and fixed to a building, &c., against the Statute.—2nd COUNT, stating it to be the property of Mary Ann Pierce Hamilton.
JOSEPH HENRY ROBERTS . I live up a passage, between Nos. 407 and 408, Oxford-street. This gate was at the entrance of that passage—I had seen if safe at twelve o'clock on Saturday night, the 12th of March—it was brought back to me on Wednesday, between eight and nine o'clock, within three quarters of an hour after the prisoner was taken in charge.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Who was it brought back by? A. We cannot tell—it was brought after the prisoner was in custody, and put in the passage.
RICHARD MARKRAM ( police-constable C 129.) I was on duty on the Sunday night—I met the prisoner at twenty minutes before one o'clock", with this gate—I am sure he is the man I stopped—I can swear that is the gate that he had—I took him back to a light, and examined the gate—it has been mended at the bottom—I took notice of that at the time—the prisoner said he had bought it at a marine-store shop.
Cross-examined. Q. This occurred about one o'clock? A. Yes, before one o'clock—I did not take him into custody—he gave me the gate, and, through his appearance, I gave it him back again—I looked at it, and had it in my possession, perhaps a couple or three minutes—I could not recollect any thing about it, or he should not gave gone further with it—he was taken on Tuesday night—I took particular notice of him, in order that if he was taken I should know him again—I took him to a light at a tobacconist's—he went into the tobacconist's and bought a cigar, while I looked at the gate—that is about 150 yards from, where the gate was missed—I took him into custody in Regent-street, with a girl—about half a mile from where the gate was missed—I could swear to the prisoner among ten thousand—he has a very particular look in his eye and his features, that I know him again.
COURT. Q. It was on Sunday night you let him go? A. Yes; I saw him to Tuesday night, as I was going on duty, at twenty minutes before eight o'clock.
Q. Had he an opportunity of communicating with any body after you took him, till the gate came back? A. No; there was a girl with him—she went to the watch-house, and then I sent her away, after she had been there two or three minutes—he said, when she was going out, "Go and tell father and brother, you know."
Prisoner. I have got a witness to prove I was at home and in bed at the time they accuse me of the robbery.
EDWARD HUGHES . I keep a tobacconist's shop. I recollect the policeman coming in with the gate, and a person who resembles the prisoner, but I cannot swear to him—I noticed the gate, and can swear to it.
CATHERINE BROWN . I have lodged with the prisoner's mother upwards of two years—he lodges there like wise—that is at No. 21, Vine-street, Inquorpond-street, but they do not go by that name—on Sunday, the 13th of March, I was at home, getting my supper, at half-past ten o'clock, and saw George and Henry Baker go past my room—I was at home at eight o'clock—I saw the prisoner, and his father and mother, sitting down to supper, about a quarter before ten o'clock—I saw the prisoner go past my room at half-past ten o'clock—he wished me good night, and went to their room, which adjoins mine—he had no gate with him—I have known
him six years—his father is a very severe kind of man, and never suffers his children out of his place—I am positive he would not have allowed him to go out after that.
COURT. Q. You saw him pass your door at half-past ten o'clock—that is all you know about it? A. Yes; I did not see him in bed.
JURY. Q. What was to prevent him going out? A. I did not say he did not go out, but his father would not allow him.
GEORGE BAKER . I am the prisoner's father. On Sunday, the 13th of March, I solemnly declare he was in bed at half-past ten o'clock—I went to bed at a quarter before eleven o'clock—I saw him in bed then.
COURT. Q. What are you? A. A linen-weaver by trade, but I do not work at it—I am a porter to an upholsterer now—I have worked for Edward Stolworthy twelve years, and I am in his employment now.
JURY. Q. Did you and your son sleep in the same bed? A. No—I was not asleep at twelve o'clock, nor half-past twelve—I must solemnly swear he did not get up, nor did any person in my house—I was awake till half-past twelve o'clock—I was not at one o'clock—my house is situated at No. 21, Lewes-buildings, Liquorpond-street—I have a clock close to my ear—I burn a rush-light, and at half-past twelve o'clock, I looked at clock.
COURT. Q. How do you happen to remember all this so particularly? A. In consequence of his being taken up the Tuesday night after.
JURY. Q. Are you certain you were in the room at half-past twelve o'clock? A. Yes; he could not have been out without my knowing it—I went to bed at a quarter before eleven o'clock, and about half-past twelve o'clock I dropped off to sleep—when the prisoner was brought up to Marlborough-street, the policeman brought a woman, who was in the tobacconist's shop, and she looked at him, and said that was not the man.
RICHARD MARKAM re-examined. It is false—there was a woman at the back of me when we stood in the tobacconist's, as I saw there was evidence enough—I did not wish her to speak—she said he was very much like the man, but she could not swear to him.
GEORGE BAKER . When the woman not swear according to his wishes, he would not let her in—I endeavoured to find where she lived—they could find her, but I could not, and they would not let her in.
NOT GUILTY .

979. ANN CLARK was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of March, 1 cloak, value 5 s.; 1 shawl, value 2 s.; the goods of Rebecca White; 1 cloak, value 5 s.; the goods of Michael Shay; 1 shawl, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of Ellen Mahoney; 1 shawl, value 3 s., the goods of William Brown; and one shawl, value 3 s., the goods of Caroline Pinkney.
PHILIPPA GRINDELL . I live in Great Portland-street, my husband is an upholsterer. The prisoner came to me to be employed as a needle-woman, on the 18th of March, about nine o'clock in the morning—I employed her till about half-past ten o'clock, when I missed her out of the room—when the work-people were going, we found she had taken every shawl and bonnet out of the place—I never saw her again till the Monday following, when I saw her in Regent-street, looking into a window—I followed her till I sent for an officer, and had her taken.
REBECCA WHITE . This cloak belongs to me. I work at Mrs. Grindell's, and this shawl is mine—they were both gone.
ANN BROWN . This is my shawl, I lost it from there.
CAROLINE PINKEY . This shawl is mine, that was gone that day.
HENRY PINKNEY . I am a messenger at Marlborough-street-office I found the duplicates of these things on the prisoner, and I gave notice to the pawnbrokers, and the articles were all produced at the office—I bring them here—this shawl I found on the prisoner—they were pledged at diferent times and different places.
Prisoner. I was very much distressed.
GUILTY. Aged 23.—Recommended to mercy. — Confined Six moths.
[trialAccount t18360404-980]
980. SAMUEL HOLDHAM was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of March, 2 pieces of board, value 1 s. 8 d.; and 1 piece of wood, value 4 d.; the goods of Robert Webb and others, his masters.
CHARLES EWE . I am foreman to Robert Webb and others, brick-makers and builders. The prisoner was in their employ—I missed two pieces if board and one piece of wood—they are here, and are my masters'.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How do you know them? A. By matching them—the prisoner is a carman—there are a great many pieces of wood in the yard—there is a thoroughfare through the yard, but not public, it is for persons who come to the brick-fields, by day-time—I cannot say to a day when I saw this wood last—Mr. Webb would rather not prosecure him—he wishes to have him back in his employ—he has been about two years there, but still this is Mr. Webb's property—it was more than twelve months in the yard—I cannot say how long it had been out of the yard—I cannot speak to a week or months—it was taken away from a pig-stye, because I knew it so well.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
WILLIAM SMITH ( police-constable N 261.) On the night of the 16th of March I saw the prisoner in Coach and Horses-lane—he had two pieces of board—I asked how he came by them—he told me he purchased it at the stone-yard, Islington—I took him to the station-house, and then he said he had picked it up in the road—a quarter of an hour after, I went to the last witness, and told him—he came and said he was persuaded it was their property.
Cross-examined. Q. What did you find on him? A. These two pieces—this one was at the pig-stye, and I brought it away, with these other pieces—it was concealed.
GUILTY. Aged 24.—Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined Five Days.

981. JANE SIMPSON was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of March, 1 cloak, value 2 l., the goods of James Priddle.
JAMES PRIDDLE . I live in Wilson-street, Gray's-inn-lane. I am a tailor. A little before day-before day-break, on Sunday morning, the 27th of March, (I had been out all night to party of friends,) I met the prisoner in Gray's-inn-lane—she prevailed on me to go home with her to her lodging, in some court in Liquorpond-street—this was about a quarter past four o'clock—I went to a room in which Ellen Cavill was in bed—I took off my cloak, and sat upon the only seat what was in the room, and she directed my attention to Ellen Cavill—I rose from my seat, and the prisoner made
off with my cloak, while I was talking to Ellen Cavill—this is my cloak.
Prisoner. They gave me the cloak—he had been in another room with me for twenty minutes, and followed me into the room where the girl was in bed—he was intoxicated. Witness. I was perfectly sober—I never gave it to her—I was not in the room ten minutes.
MARY DAVIS . I lodge at the Half-moon public-house. About seven o'clock in the morning of Sunday, the 27th of March, I saw the prisoner with this cloak in her arms—she gave it me, and asked me if I would carry it up stairs for her, while she staid at the door, but I did not—I was walking with her with the cloak, and was stopped by the two policemen.
Prisoner. I asked her to take the cloak up stairs—he had no money about him—he made me a present of the cloak till he should call for it in morning, and said he would give me 10 s.
DANIEL HUMPHREYS ( police-constable G 74.) I look Davis with the cloak—I stopped the prisoner, and asked her where the gentleman's cloak was—she said, "What do you mean?"—I said, "No nonsense, where is the cloak?"—she said, "There is it—I was going to take it to the gentleman's lodgings."
Prisoner. He told me he lodged down Eyre-street-hill.
JAMES PRIDDLE . I never told her any thing of the kind.
JURY. Q. Did you give her any money? A. No—I only entered into one room, and five minutes did not elapse before I gave the two females in charge, because this woman lodged in the same room with them—I stopped till the cloak was found, and left the party at four o'clock in the morning, and left the place I was at with the prisoner, about half past four o'clock—the cloak was found, I believe, between six and seven o'clock—this policeman is not the one I had into the room—he found it on the prisoner going Eyre-street-hill.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Six Months.

983. THOMAS ELDRIDGE was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of March, 47lbs. weight of lead, value 7 s., the goods of William Brass, and fixed to a building, against the Statute, & c.
HENRY BRASS . I am the son of William Brass, who lives in Wood-street, Cheapside, and is proprietor of a building in Essex-street, Hoxton.
There was some lead on that building—I have compared the lead found by the officer, with the lead remaining on that building—it exactly corresponds—I have no doubt it came from there.
WILLIAM HOLLAND ( police-constable N 146.) On Thursday morning, the 3rd of March, from information, I went at a quarter before six o'clock in the morning, to an unoccupied house in Essex-street—I went on the first floor, there was no one there—I went on the second floor, and found a piece of lead, and found the tiles were removed—had not been there more than a minute, when I heard some person coming up stairs—the prisoner came up first, and another was behind him—the prisoner advanced towards the lead, and said to the other one, "Come along, here it is"—he turned, and saw me and the other policeman—I caught the prisoner—the other got away—the officer pursued him, but did not take him—I took the prisoner and the lead to the station.
RICHARD HAWKES . I went on the roof of the house the lead was taken from, and saw some foot-marks—I went to the station, and took the prisoner's shoe—it fitted the marks exactly—I pursued the man who ran away—he ran through the prisoner's mother's house, which joins the premises.
GEORGE KEMP ( police-constable N 82.) I got this certificate of the prisoner's former conviction from the office of the Clerk of the Peace, at Clerkenwell ( read)—the prisoner is the person who was tried.
Prisoner's Defence. I went there to sleep, because I could not get into my mother's house.
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Transported for Seven Years.

984. SARAH FIELD was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of March, 1 spoon, value 1 s., the goods of Edward Aviolet.
EDWARD AVIOLET . I am a cork-cutter, and live at Edmonton—the prisoner was in my employ occasionally as a char-woman—she was employed on the 19th of March, and on the 20th we missed a silver spoon—I sent for her and questioned her about it—she said she did not know where it was, unless she had thrown it with the dish-wates, down the drain—I said the thing was impossible and I should send for a constable unless she acknowledge where it was—I found it at Mr. Hart's from what she told me.

AARON HART . I live at Tottenham. The prisoner brought this spoon to me to know if I would buy it, on Saturday, the 19th of March—I detained it and gave it to the officer.
JOHN WALL GRIMLEY . I am an officer. I took the prisoner.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 17.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury and the Prosecutor, who promised to employ her again. — Confined Five Days.

Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS Q. Are you in the habit of scouring blankets for St. George's Hospital? A. Yes, this is one of their blankets—I was deficient of one blanket about September, but I cannot say that this might not have been stolen from the hospital.

986. SAMUEL WOOLAWAY was again indicted for stealing, on the 18th of March, 16 gallons of dye, value 20 s.; 18lbs. of logwood, value 2 s.; 16lbs. of fustic, value 2 s.; 12lbs. of peach wood, value 2 s.; 4lbs. of bark, value 4 d.; 2 stone bottles, value 1 s.; 3 iron doors and frames, value 6 s.; 1 gas pillar and burner, value 6 s.; 1 glass tumbler, value 4 d.; 2 yards of printed cotton, value 1 s.; and 10lbs. of iron, value 6 d.; the goods of John Jones, his master.
JOHN JONES . I live in Hanway-street, and am a dyer. The prisoner was in my service up to the 18th of March—having missed several articles, I went to his lodgings in Steven-streethe told me that was where he resided—I found there this gas pillar, a quantity of dye stuff, and the other articles stated—I have every reason to believe they are mine—I can swear to the gas pillar and this copper door, by this mark—it was too tight, and hod to be altered—this piece of furniture is a paticular pattern—I can swear it is mine, and these stone bottles also.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS Q. Has he ever done work for you at home? A. No—I did not particularly advise that he should plead guilty, or that he should employ nobody to defend him—his sister asked me to be lenient with him, and I said, "Perhaps it would be better for him to plead GUILTY."
EDMUND DAVIS ( police-constable E 74.) I went to Steven-street, with Mr. Jones, and found this property.
Prisoner's Defence. The articles they found were my own—I have had that door in my possession for the last two years—there are many dresses of that pattern—I should him that glass tumbler on the floor—he then swore to it, and he swore to a picture that was found there on the first examination, and then denied it.
(Richard Ward, a gardener, of Stoke Newington; Reuben Herbert, a servant; and Elizabeth Ward, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18360404-987]
987. SAMUEL TITE and GEORGE FROST, alias Webber , were indicted for stealing, on the 6th of March, 96lbs. weight of lead, value 10 s., the goods of Abraham Gole, fixed to a certain building of his, against the Statute, &c.; and SIM JEWELL for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing the same to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
THOMAS MATHER . I live in Shadwell High-street, and am agent to Mr. Abraham Gole, who is a leaseholder of the shed in question. It is only one room—I do not know where the lead was taken from.
CHARLES GROVE TAYLOR . I am servant to Mr. Thomas Grout, who keeps a lodging-house, at No. 5, Gower's Mill-yard. The prisoners Tite and Frost work there—they slept there on a Saturday night, about the 5th or 6th of March—I got up two o'clock in the morning, as I generally do—I found Frost asleep in a bed, and Tite, who was dressed, by the kitchen fire—I asked Tite if he was not going to bed—he said he did not know, as
they had to be out so early—I went by direction of the superintendent of the house to call Frost at five o'clock in the morning—he did not come down—I was sent again, but he did not come—I was told to go a third time—I said I had been often enough—Tithe then said, "If he does not come down before six o'clock, it will be 15 s. or 16 s. out of his pocket"—I then took Tite up with me to where Frost was asleep—I just touched Frost, and he said, "I will be down directly"—it was then between five and six o'clock—they came down, and as they were going out, Frost wanted to borrow 1 1/2 d. on a knife, to get some gin—he did say any thing about having no money.
Q. Recollect whether you did not tell the Magistrate that he said he had no money? A. I cannot say particular whether he did or not—I gave him one halfpenny, and a person in the room gave him a penny.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS Q. Who is the superintendent? A. Mr. Baylis—I go to bed at seven o'clock, and get up at two o'clock in the morning—when I came down, I found Tithe by the fire-side fast asleep—that is not an unusual thing in out house—I have known Frost by him coming backwards and forwards for two or three months.
HENRY THOMAS MALLEY ( police-sergeant K 23.) I was at the station-house on Sunday morning, the 6th of March, and about eleven o'clock, the prisoner Frost, who gave his name "Webber," came there in a state of intoxication—he said he had met with a man the day before who had robbed him of 2 l. 5 s —I asked him if he knew the man—he said, no he had never seen him till the day before—that he had promised to get him work, and he had employed him to carry some lead for him, and he was to give him 5 s. for carrying it—I asked him if he knew where-the lend came from—he said he could point out the place—I went with him to the place, in Shadwell-market—it is a sort of sentit is an unoccupied place—the door was standing open, and, on looking up to the ceiling, I saw some lead had been taken off.
THOMAS MATHER . That place is the property of Abraham Gole, Esq.—I have known it for twenty years.
Cross-examined. Q. Is Mr. Gole alive? A. Yes—it is in consequence of having seen some leases, and having the rent to receive, and the taxes and repairs to pay that—I know the premises well—they are in my possession at this time.
Q. Was there any lead whatever, to your knowledge, on that building? A. Not to my knowledge—and in the course of twenty years it must want repairing—I never had to repair any lead—I never say any lead on that building in my life—I never knew what it was covered with—it is only one room—the roof may be ten or twelve feet high—it was hardly ever used—I could not let it at all—I have never had to repair it—I saw it covered—I saw the tiles put on—these was a gutter, but I do not know what it was made of—I never saw the gutter.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You know this is place was covered with tiles? A. Yes, that is true.
HENRY THOMAS MALLEY re-examined. Q. Have you been to a house of shed in Shadwell-market? A. Yes, it was formerly a market-house—it is a place without any first-floor, and there is a back-door blocked up—the house next to it is a corner house, and occupied by Irish people—I believe it is Cross-street, but is very little knownon the other side are two or three small houses, leading to Riley's beer-shop—there is only one room to this, and all the others in the street have two or three rooms—I
went with Frost inside the place, and saw the door standing open—I saw a hole in the roof through the ceiling—It seemed to have been recently made—I then went outside—I looked on the parapet, and saw the states, which were moved from the top, laid on the coping—there was a gutter there, and I subsequently found the remains of lead in that gutter which leads to the next house—I afterwards found some other lead, which I fitted to the lead in the gutter—It fitted exactly—I have not the slightest doubt that one was taken from the other.
THOMAS MATHER re-examined. to the best of my belief the building he describes is the house I have been receiving rent for, for the last twenty years, but there are a great many houses in the same square with only one room—whether the lead came from there I cannot say.
Cross-examined. Q. Are there in that street other places with only one room? A. Certainly—there is no back-door to this place, either blocked up or open—there never was a back-door to that house—never at all—I have known it ever since it was built, and saw it building—I swear it never had a back-door, or I must have seen it—this place is covered with tiles—there were no slates on it to my knowledge—if the policeman says that the house from which the lead was taken had a back-door blocked up, it certainly cannot be Mr. Goles's house.
COURT. Q. Have you been to this house since the charge has been made against the prisoner? A. Certainly—I went by myself—I found a hole in the roof.
HENRY THOMAS MALLEY re-examined. Q. Had the house the appearance of having a door out behind? A. Yes the appearance of a way out, blocked up with boards—there are boards at the back more than twice my height, and all the way up is boards—I thought there was a door-way, and laid hold of a place—I could not get to the back, it was so blocked up—it is only one room—there are no windows in it, but I think four shutten at the front part—there is one door on the right-hand side as you go out of the alley—you go past the first shop at the corner—it is the second door from the alley, on the right hand.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you not say corner there was a back-door blocked up? A. Yes.
THOMAS MATHER . That is the house I receive the rent of; but there never was a back-door—the house is built with those boards at the back.
HENRY THOMAS MALLEY . There is a sort of post up, which made me take it for a door—I went there on Sunday morning, the 6th of March, at a quarter-past eleven o'clock, with the prisoner Frost—I asked if he wood point out where the lead was taken from—he pointed up to the hole in the roof, and said, "There is where it came from"—I did not go on the roof then; but there was an appearance of lead having been removed, from the slates being moved—I then went with Frost to a marine-store shop in Shadwell, kept by a brother of the prisoner Jewell—the name of Joel Jewell is over the door—I there saw the prisoner Sim Jewell, and his brother Sam Jewell—I asked them where the lead was that they had bought of the man Webber (Frost) and another man that morning—they both said they had bought no lead—I said I was quite certain that lead had been brought there, and I would not go away till I was satisfied—Webber said, "That won's do; this is the place where it was brought to"—Sim Jewell then took me up two pair of stairs to a back room, and there shewed me some lead which came from tea-chests—he said, "That is all the lead we have got"—Webber said, "That won't do; this is the place
we brought it to: I know the lead is about the place"—we then went down into the shop—Sam and Sim Jewell were both there, and I said, "If you have bought this lead, and deny it in this manner, it will go very much against you afterwards"—I found some lead in the back warehouse, between some bags of rags—I had it conveyed to the station-house, and then took Sim Jewell into custody—I went, in consequence of something which Samuel Jewell said, to a street in the New-road, where I found the prisoner Tite—I told him he was charged with stealing some lead the night before, and he must come with me to the station-house—he said he knew nothing about any lead—Webber was with me in Tite's room—there came a knock at the door, and I went down, leaving my brother constable in the room with them—when they were in the cells, I said to Tite, "You are charged with stealing some lead from the roof of a house"—he turned round, and, pointing to Webber, he said to Sim Jewell, "It was him that carried the land and sold it to your brother Sam, was not it?"—Jewell said, "Yes"—Jewell was then sent down to the other station-house, and the other two prisoner were locked in different cells; and in a short time I heard some talking between them—I went to the cell where Webber was, and said, "What are you talking about here?"—Webber said that Tithe had told him, if he would not swear to him, he would give him back the 2 l. 5 s. which his wife had got—Tite could hear any question, and could hear the answer—I shut the small door, and went in again—I then went to Jewell's house, and fetched some lead or pewter, I do not know which, which appeared recently melted down—one of the pieces was quite hot.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You any Webber was intoxicated? A. He was at eleven o'clock in the morning—Tite said he knew nothing about it in the first instance, and afterwards he acknowledged to being along with Webber when he carried it.
Q. Did you ever say here to-day that he was along with Webber when he carried it? A. I said that he said to Jewell. "It was him that carried the lead, and sold it to your brother Sam, was not it?"—I have told you his exact words—he did not say at that time that he was along with him.
JURY. Q. Was it from his words you drew the inference? A. Yes.
Frost. Q. When did you see me first? Witness A. About eleven o'clock that morning, at Shadwell—you were drunk—I did not drink with you to make you drunker, to my knowledge—I did not go into a public-house, and call for a not of beer—I did not know Tite till after I had been to Jewell's I took Webber with me—he had then got sober—he was with me from eleven o'clock in the morning till three in the afternoon—he had 9 1/2 d., and I allowed him to have some beer—he begged very hard.
COURT. Q. When was the public-house he had it at? A. I rather think it was at the corner of Grove-street—upon my oath I did not drink with either of the prisoners after taking them into custody—they offered me the pot, but I did not drink.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. What time did you go to the public-house? A. It was about three o'clock, after I had taken Tite—Webber was sober then; as far as a man could be that had been tipsy—I suppose 4 d. was paid for the liquor—I did not see it paid—9 1/2 d. was taken from him at the King David-land station—I cannot tell the sign of the public-house—it was the first time I ever was in it—I was not on duty at the time—the man begged hard to have a drop of beer, and I allowed him to have it—there were three of them—there was a friend of Tite's.
Frost. You paid for a pot of beer, and drank with me before you had Tite—you drank, and offered me the pot, and said, "Drink"—I said, "You have been a rascal, you have made me drunker"—I would not drink with you. Witness. I never saw you till you was drunk—I did not make him more drunk than he was—I did not allow him to drink till three o'clock in the afternoon.
HENRY PARKER ( police-constable K 156.) I went with Malley to apprehend Tite—he said, "I should like to know what I am charged with before I go"—he had been previously charged with lead, but this was while Malley was down stairs—Tite was sitting on the side of the bed, dressing himself—I said it was for some lead taken from shadwell-market—he said, "As far as regards the robbery, I know nothing at all about it; but I met Frost in Ratcliff-highway."
JAMES FOGG . I am a Thames-police surveyor. I was passing Jewell's shop on Sunday morning, the 6th of March, at about eight o'clock—I saw Tite come from Jewell's, and go into a public-house, a few doors further down—there was another man with him, but not Webber.
Frost's Defence. I was employed as a porter, to carry it to Jewell's—I did not know what it was—I thought I was taken for being drunk.
TITE— GUILTY [Recommended to mercy. See original trial image.] . Aged 30.
FROST — GUILTY. Aged 21. Recommended to mercy .— Confined One Year.
JEWELL— NOT GUILTY .

988. WILLIAM FRANKLIN was indicted for stealing. on the 1st of March, 1 wooden post, value 1 s. 6 d., the goods of the Company of Proprietors of the Grand Junction Canal.
THOMAS HARDING . I live at Appleton, in Middlesex, and am overseer of the Grand Junction Canal. I went to examine the bridges over the cannal, near the Wagon and Horses, and missed forty feet six inches, in one length of post—this is some of the property—we found fifteen feet six inches—it is the property of the Grand Junction Canal Company—it was fixed—I got our carpenter, and had every thing fitted, and this is part of it—it was fitted in before the prisoner—I have the Act of Parliament incorporating the Company.
WILLIAM FAIR . I am a horse-patrol of Bow-street. I was at Hayes on the 1st of March, at eight o'clock, and met the prisoner with a large rail on his shoulders, I asked where he brought it from—he said from a house close by—I asked him to point out the house, he then said, he found it in the road—I matched it the next morning with that on the canal, and found it tallied exactly—I then found another piece in a farm-yard.
ROBERT ROWLAND . I am a carpenter. I have fitted this rail to that by the side of the canal, I know it is the property of the Canal Company.
Prisoner. I am not guilty of stealing, if I am guilty of picking it up.
JURY to THOMAS HARDING. Q. When did you see the railling last? A. On the Saturday, and it was stolen on the Monday—it was all sound then—it had not been up above a year and a half—and it was sawed off in two places.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Two Months.

989. THOMAS LOVE and ELIZA HAVILL were indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 15th of March, 6 yards of woollen cloth, value 3 l., the goods of Robert Morrison, well knowing them to have been stolen, against the Statute, &c.
ROBERT MORRISON . I keep a shop in High-street, Borough, and another in St. Paul's Church-yard. I had nine yards of green cloth in my shop, in the Borough—I did not send it there—it was it in the Borough after it had been sent—we have it entered in the book there—this is part of the cloth—we took stock there in February, which was sinece the robsince—I am able to swear it was stolen from there—I have some portions of it still remaining at St. Paul's.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How long before had you seen the entire piece of cloth safe? A. In the month of December, in St. Paul's Church-yard—I have seen that portion of it which was sent to the ware-house, in the Borough—it was there in December—I have here to-day all the persons in the warehouse, in the Borough, except Love, who has been accused, and has escaped—he had an opportunity of stealing it—was suspect he stole it, but we cannot prove it—he had been in the habit of working for me, and coming on the premises to fetch work—I don't know that he had not the opportunity of stealing it—he worked for me for about a month.
JOHN DENYER . I work for Mr. Morrison. The prisoner Love was employed by him—he left about five months back—I cannot swear when this cloth was last seen, but I saw it in December last, while Love was working there, but not where the cloth was—he took his work out—Bells was a shopman, and there are two lads—I have seen this bag in our shop—it is the property of Mr. Morrison.
Cross-examined. Q. The person who had the opportunity of stealing the cloth could also have stolen the bag? A. He might have done so.
GEORGE HODGES . I am shopman to James Henry Ashton, of Long-acre, a pawnbroker. I have a coat pledged by the female prisoner.
NOT GUILTY .

[trialAccount t18360404-991]
991. MARGARET RYAN was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of March, 2 purses, value 16 d.; 4 sovereigns, 2 half-crowns, and 7 shillings.; the goods and monies of Charles Shepherd, from his person.
CHARLES SHEPHEARD . I live in Barbican. On Thursday last, the 31st of March, I had been dining with a few friends, and had taken a glass of wine too much—I met the prisoner close by my own door, and was allured away to a house of ill-fame—I forget the name of the court I went to, but the officer knows—she robbed me of this money, and two purses—four sovereigns, seven shillings, and two half-crowns, that was in a red and brown purse, and there was another purse in my other pocket, empty—she had an opportunity of taking it, for the officer found a portion of the money on her the following morning—I was not with her more than three minutes—I did not pay her—I was to have done so, but I
could not after she robbed me—there was not the least dispute about it—I am sure she is the person—I was not always so certain—I should be very sorry to be too positive against the prisoner; only from the officer finding on her a part of my property, but I am sure I should have recollected her from her dialect alone—she is Irish.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Had you occasion to use your purse before you left your friends? A. Yes—before I left the College of physicians—I left there about eleven o'clock—I think I can be certain I spoke to no one before I spoke to her—I had spoken to some girls before I met her—I was to have given her 5 s.—I am certain I did not give her all I had in my pocket—she shut the door, and locked me in—I felt her take it—I felt her hand at my pocket, and she made an immediate rush at the door.
COURT. Q. Did you say this before the Magistrate? A. I answered the questions that were put to me.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Then to the Magistrate you never gave an account of this? A. No—it was at the time I was toying with her that I felt her take the money—I could not detain her—she shut the door very suddenly—I certainly took the purse to the house with me, and took it out, and paid 1 s. for the room.
Prisoner. He met me in Barbican, and forced me into a room, and then the landlady asked him to pay her, he said he would when he came down stairs, and she said he should find some other lodging—he paid her 1 s., and the he was an hour and a half in the room with me.
EDWARD M'DONALD ( City police-constable No. 78.) I received information, went to the prisoner's house, and found seven duplicates and a purse, which belongs to the prosecutor, I believe, and half-a-crown, and 1 1/2 d.
CHARLES SHEPHERD . This is my purse—these duplicates are mine, which I am in the habit of purchasing—I do not always get my livelihood so—I only buy them of persons I know.
Cross-examined. Q. How far is this house of ill-fame from your own house? A. Only a dew yards—I am certain I was not talking to a man at the time I saw this girl—I had been to the College of Physicians's Arms—I had not been drinking all day—I was quite capable to taking care of myself.
NOT GUILTY .
Sixth Jury, Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
