1013. WILLIAM HEWLETT was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of March, 1 pair of half-boots, value 3 s., the goods of Samuel Woolstone.
SAMUEL WOOLSTONE . I live in High-street, St. Giles's and am a cordwainer—I lost this pair of half-boots, on the 13th of March, about half-past seven o'clock, or between seven and wight o'clock—they were hanging just inside the door-post—I had not told them.
PETER YOUNGMAN . About five minutes before seven o'clock in the evening. 1 was in High-street, St. Giles's—I saw the prisoner steal from the shop of Mr. Woolstone a pair of half-boots—he ran from the shop—I directly pursued and caught him—he ran and tried to make his escape—I saw him drop one when the officer took him—I did not see him drop the other, but I heard it fall—the officer picked up one, and I believe some one else picked up one.
HENRY CURTIS ( police-constable F 156.) I saw the prisoner running, and just as the man caught him, I saw him drop one boot—I picked up this, and a person just by me gave me the other.
Prisoner. I took from distress—my father would not give me a farthing.
GUILTY . Aged 29.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18360404-1014]
1014. MARGARET QUEENY was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 1 pair of trowsers, value 2 s. 6 d.; 1 waistcoat, value 1 s. 6 d.; and 1 handkerchief. value 1 s.; the goods of Patrick M'Carthy.
CATHERINE M'CARTHY . I live in Prospect-place, Limehouse. I went to the Jolly Sailors public-house to sell oysters—the prisoner asked me, being a countrywoman, to drink with her, which I did—we came away about two o'clock in the day—she said, did I know where she could lodge—I said, no, I had no bed, but that on which my husband and me slept—she said she would make any shift—I said, if I could hear of any place, I would let her know—I went out and met her again about four o'clock, and the she asked me again—I had not the heart 10 refuse her, having a young baby in her arms—I took her in, and had a little soup for my husband's supper—I left her a little of it—I went out a little while, and while I was gone she went away with these things—the woman saw her going and followed her, and then fetched my husband and me—we followed her and husband sent for on officer.
WILLIAM CHARLES ELLIS . I live with Mr. Duker, a pawnbroker, of Limehouse. I produce a pair of trowsers, a waistcoat, and handkerchief, pledged by the prisoner.
CATHERINE M'CARTHY . These are my husband's—his name is Patrick.
Prisoner. I did not pawn these things.
WILLIAM CHARLES ELLIS . I am sure she is the person—she had the child in her arms, which she has now.
Prisoner. I did it out distress.
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18360404-1015]
1015. WILLIAM FITT , the younger, was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of March, 4 half-crowns, 1 shilling, and 2 pence; the monies of William Fitt.
WILLIAM FITT, SEN . I am the father of this boy. On the 10th of March I was awoke about three o'clock in the morning by one of my daughters—I got out of bed to ask what she was crying for—she said the prisoner and another had gone and left her—I went down and found the street door open—I came up again, dressed myself, and missed from my breeches pocket four half-crowns and one shilling—I went out and found both my boys at the corner of Spital-square, in Bishopgate-street—I took the little one home, and gave the prisoner in charge, as he has robbed me so often—he has got a bad mother, who encourages him in it—she is in the hospital, and I will not let the children go near her—she has got property of mine, and will not give it up—she is his own mother—he had got the money in his pocket, in a tin box.
JURY. Q. Do you and your wife live together? A. Not for these last six months—he has taken money from me many times—he has taken things to his mother, an old watch I had, and a gold ring—I sent him to school, and he has left it—she is not a woman of bad habits otherwise—she has encouraged the boy to rob—we have been married fifteen years last November, and we separated six month ago—he robbed me before many times, and I have corrected him for it—his mother did not encourage him then, but she has since she left me.
GUILTY. Aged 12.— Judgment Respited.
[trialAccount t18360404-1016]
1016. HUGH MORRIS was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of March, 120 lbs. of lead, value 25 s., the goods of John Perkins.
GEORGE JAMES BRAM . I am a cabinet-maker, and live in James's-place, Lower-road, Islington; in consequence of what my apprentice told me, I went to a wall in my premises, and saw the prisoner in the act of stamping up three pieces of lead—he was then in the back kitchen of an uninhabited house, belonging to Mr. Perkins—I have always understood it was his—I saw the prisoner, after he stamped up two small pieces, put them up the chimney—I then went and fetched my lodger, and told him to watch while I went to give information to Mr. Wrigglesworth—but before I went I saw him stamp up another piece, and put it in the jambs of the chimney—I went and got Mr. Wrigglesworth, and brought him—the prisoner was in my house, in company with two others—I keep a beer shop there—Mr. Wrigglesworth came with a policeman, and while one was making his escape, the prisoner got from Mr. Wrigglesworth and ran away—I pursued him, and then returned and got the lead.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. After you saw this lead put up the chimney, the prisoner came into your house? A. Yes—there might be ten or a dozen persons drinking there—I will not swear whether the prisoner was sober—I saw no one with him in the uninhabited house—he had a white jacket on—there was no one else in the kitchen.
GEORGE BOLTON . I am apprentice to Mr. Bray. About half-past three o'clock in the afternoon, I saw another man give the prisoner the lead—he doubled it up, and put it up the chimney.
Cross-examined. Q. How far off were you? A. I was standing at the top of the door, perhaps six yards off—my master came about half-past three o'clock—the prisoner was then doubling up the lead—these two men and the prisoner remained in the place about a quarter of an hour—after master came—the other man went away just before master came—the beer-shop is on the same premises as the cabinet-making—I had seen the prisoner drinking there that morning a good while—I cannot say whether he was sober when I saw him with the lead—after that he came over the wall into the beer shop again—he turned round and saw me while he was doing it—he put it up the chimney five minutes after he saw me—he then came into our beer shop, and then went up to the gate with two more—he staid there about two minutes.
JOHN WRIGGLESWORTH . I am in the employ of Mr. John Perkins. I received information which induced me to go to this house in the Lower Road—I saw one piece of lead in the chimney-jamb, standing as Mr. Bray had given me information—I saw the prisoner coming out of the garden belonging to the premises—I got the officer—he followed, and took him as he was crossing the road—the house in Mr. Perkins's.
Cross-examined. Q. How do you know it is his? A. I have had the superintendance and building of it for Mr. Perkins—I have paid of for it as his agent.
GEORGE BROWN . I am a plumber. A piece of lead was left on the top of the roof of this house, and we missed it—I live at Ann's Place, Hoxton—I was employed to do his work for Mr. Pearson—it is Mr. Perkins's lead.
Cross-examined. Q. Are there any marks on it? A. Yes, it is lined with a tape-line, and cut with a knife.
JOHN KIDLEY . I work for Mr. Pearson—I know this other piece of lead—I left it on the house.
EDWARD FLAGG ( police-constable N 83.) I took the prisoner after he ran away another person.
WILLIAM EPPS ( police-constable N 168.) I found the lead up the chimney.
Prisoner. I am quite innocent—I had been drinking the whole of the morning in this witness's beer shop, with three or four more—the place was quite open for any body to go there.
GEORGE BOLTON re-examined. I am quite sure that these men were the persons I saw in the building with two others—I did not count how many persons were in the bar from three o'clock to half-past three—it was not full—there were not twenty persons—the other man that I saw giving him the lead had corderoy things on—there was another had a white flannel jacket, besides the prisoner—I cannot say what became of him—it was the prisoner took the lead—his back was turned towards me, but I saw him get over the wall—I saw his face for about two minutes.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 30.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18360404-1017]
1017. THOMAS MASON. alias Hefferman , was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of George Birdseye, from his person.
GEORGE BIRDSEYE . I was in Gracechurch-street—on the 6th of April, about a quarter-past nine o'clock, I observed three or four boys—I knew I was surrounded by thieves, and I rather encouraged the young one who was behind me to take my handkerchief—walking along, and finding I was followed, I thought by giving him in custody I might do good—I walked rather slowly—I felt my pocket, and my handkerchief was gone—I had not felt it taken—I turned and saw it in the prisoner's hand—he dropped it and ran away—I pursued him some distance, and called, "Stop thief!"—he ran up Corbett-court, which is no thoroughfare; and there I took him, and picked the handkerchief up.
Prisoner. My master was in the country—I was going to see if he was come home, and was running to make haste—there were three boys behind him; but I did not see the handkerchief, Witness. I did not see the handkerchief in his hand, but he dropped it behind him—I chose rather to take the actual thief, and not the others—they were two or three yards from him—I am able to swear that the prisoner dropped it, and no one else—the other boys were bigger—they were not near enough to take it.
GEORGE BRADDING . I am a watchman of Gracechurch-street—I took the prisoner into custody.
JOSEPH WALKER . I was constable of the night—the prisoner was brought to the watch-house.
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18360404-1018]
1018. JOSIAH DOWLING was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of March, 1 shilling, the money of George Cutter.
GEORGE CUTLER . I live in Sidney-grove. Sidney-street. On Saturday evening, the 5th of March, I was in the City Arms, City-road—Richard Bunting and Josiah Dowling came and sat there; Bunting then brought in a petition to raise a subscription to bury Joseph Erby's child—I gave him two sixpences towards it—I put it down on the table, and Dowling took it up—while we stopped there Bunting's master sent for him—he asked not to go with Dowling, and try to get what I could for the child; which I did—the prisoner was to give that shilling to Erby for me.
JOSEPH ERBY . I had the misfortune to lose a child—I knew of Dowling and Bunting going about to get money for me—I gave the petition into their hands—Dowling has not given me a shilling received from the last witness, for my child—I made no application to him for it—I went to the officer—if he has received any for me, he has not paid it.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18360404-1019]
1019. DEBORAH BIRMINGHAM was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of March, 1 watch value 2 l.; and 1 watch-key, value 1 l.; the goods of Thomas Henry Sheldon, from his person; and MARIA HOLLINSON for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen, &c., against the Statute.
THOMAS HENRY SHELDON . I live in Oxford-street. I was a publican, but am now out of business. At half-past twelve o'clock on the night of the 30th of March, the prisoner Birmingham ran after me in Oxford-street, and asked me to treat her—the public-house were shut up—she asked me to go to a friend of her's which I did—that was at the corner of Crown-street, Soho—knowing her as an old customer, we went up-stairs—I was in the room about seven minutes—she was with me all the time—I was not quite sober—there was 1 s. given for some drink—that
was all I had—I had only that shilling, and it was given to get something to drink—I believe I gave it to the girl—I do not know whether the room was to be paid for—in a little time I found I had lost my watch—I accused Birmingham of stealing it—she declared she had not got it—she was in the room all the time—no one else was there—she said, after a little while, that if I would give her a sovereign she would find the watch; she dare say it was on the other side of the room—we looked about, and could not find it; we had been there ten minutes, waiting for the liquor to come—I had not got a sovereign—I sent for a policeman, and searched the place; and a person came and searched the prisoner—the property was not found—she then went away, and the next day I issued some bills; and on Saturday I found the watch pledged in Charlotte-street, New-cut; and from information, we traced Birmingham to live somewhere in Westminster—on the Monday morning I went down with a police officer, and met her—I gave her into custody—I had been spending the evening in Tottenham-court-road with a number of friends—I left them about twelve o'clock—no one could have had this watch from me before I got to Crown-street—a moment before I went into the house I looked at the watch, and it was twenty minutes past twelve o'clock.
Birmingham. He paid 6 d. for the room, and offered me 6 d.—I did not think it worth while to tale that—I was going away—he then accused me of stealing his watch; and on the Thursday a young girl he had been with before brought me this watch, which my aunt pledged for her—there was a stout girl walking with him in Soho-square. Witness. There was no other girl with me that evening.
ROBERT COCKS . I am shopman to Mr. Farrand, a pawnbroker. On the morning of the 31st of March, Hollinson brought this watch to me—she said it was her husband's, and her name was Jones—that she lived in Pebber-street—I was not satisfied—I watched her, and when she got a short distance, the other prisoner crossed to her, and she gave something into Birmingham's hand—I asked her back—she came, and the other prisoner came back to my door—I said, "Where is the money?"—Birmingham then gave the money back—I found it was right—I then sent a young man with her, to see if she lived as the stated, at No. 23, Pebber-street—he gave her the money, and came back—it was in consequence of what I said they were given into custody.
WILLIAM HEAFORD . I met Mr. Sheldon on the 12th of April, and from information we went and found the watch in Charlotte-street, New-cut—we then went to Pebber-street, but could not find the parties; and then we went to where Hollinson said she lived—we went to No. 25, Pebber-street—I am sure of that—we got information there, and went to New Pye-street, with the woman belonging to No.25, Pebber-street—I went up-stairs, with the aunt of these two prisoners, and saw them sitting at tea—I had nobody there but myself—I said "I want you about the watch"—Birmingham said, What watch?—Is the watch found?"—I said, "Yes"—" Who does it belong to?" said she—I said, "Sheldon"—they began to cry, and said what could I do—I said I could do nothing—I would see Sheldon—Birmingham gave me twelve shillings and some duplicates—I said "I will mind these;" I look it to Mr. Sheldon, and he said I had done wrong—and on the Monday I went with him there, and took it back.
Birmingham. I gave you twenty-nine duplicates and twelve shillings—you then asked the landlady if she could let you have a sovereign—I took my things to pawn—I took even my stays, and he said that would do—you
said if I made you up 1 l. of the money, you would take the red on yourself—you even stood sixpennyworth of gin, and said you would see what you could do. Witness. She did send some things to pawn—I was in a very curious situtaion, in a house full of girls and thieves—I could not do as I liked there—Mr. Sheldon said he would not have any thing to do with it—I said I would see what I could do, when she gave me the money.
JURY Q. Did you tell her she had better make it up? A. No. she asked what I could do, and I said I would see.
GEORGE PALMER ( police-sergeant E 17.) on the 4th of March I apprehended the prisoner Birmingham—she said she sis not steal the watch—that she had it from Fat Ann.
Birmingham's Defence. Last Thursday week the girl came and asked if I would pawn the watch—I said I would not—my aunt said she would take it—I saw nothing more of her till Mr. Sheldon came to me—then this man said, could I make up 1 l.—I then took all my things to pawn—I made up 12 s., and gave him twenty-mine duplicates.
JURY to T. H. SHELDON. Q. Was this friend of yours appointed to go about this business? A. I met him on Saturday morning, and stated my loss—I asked him to go to assist me in finding it—I did not allow, or hint to him, that if he could get it back, or get a small sum towards it, he was to take it—when he brought this money I said I would have nothing to do with it—I would punish her as far as the law would allow.
NOT GUILTY .

1019. HANNAH PAYNE was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of April, 1 writing-desk, value 10 s., the goods of Richard Macdonald Shouls.
RICHARD MACDONALD SHOULS . I live in Cullum-street, Fenchurch-street. I had a desk there—I received information, and missed it on the 3rd of April—this is it.
SAMUEL MORTIMER . I am in the service of the landlord of the Brown Bear—on Sunday afternoon, the 3rd of April, I heard a noise in one of the boxes in the tap-room—I went and saw the prisoner with this desk, trying to open it with a nail—I took it from her, and gave her in charge.
WILLIAM DICKENSON . I was sent for to the public-house in Rosemary-lane last Sunday—I found the prisoner, and took her into custody, with this desk.
Prisoner's Defence. I was in distress—I saw the door open, and went in and took it.

1020. GUSTAVUS HALLENBURGH was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of February, 1 watch, value 1 l. 5 s., the goods of John Pritchard.
JOHN PRITCHARD . I had a slight knowledge of the prisoner's brother, who keeps a foreign bookseller's shop in King William-street. I saw the prisoner there, and conceiving him to be a fine buy, and speaking the French and German languages, 1 thought to do something for him—he came to my house on the 9th of February, and requested me to go to Southampton-row, which I did—he requested me to give him a sheet of paper, which I did, and left him in my room—my watch was on the mantel-piece—I returned in about ten minutes—he was gone, and the watch also—there was nobody in my apartment but him—the people who
keep the house are respectable—there is an old gentleman, his daughter and niece, and the servant.
Prisoner. Q. Did you I ask you for the paper? Witness. A. Yes; and I gave it you out of a drawer—I put it on the table—there might have been two or three things on the table—I am not aware that there was any thing else there.
CATHERINE CAME . I am servant to Mr. Beal, of Brunswick-row, Queen-square. The prosecutor lives there—I remember the prisoner coming there that day—I let him in when he knocked at the door—I came down stairs with a pail of water—in about five minutes after I came past Mr. pritchard's room, and the door was ajar—shortly after wards there came a knock at the street door, and it was Mr. pritchard—the prisoner was then gone.
Prisoner. Q. You heard me first knock at the door? Witness. A. Yes—I heard the door open afterwards and shut—I was in the room over where you were—if any one had gone out again I should have heard it—you did not go out at the time Mr. Pritchard did, because I looked out of the window.
Prisoner. I could not get a situation, as I could not get a character—I went to the prosecutor to ask him to go to my brother, to ask him to give me a character—he said he would, and we left the house together—I know nothing about the watch—the servant says, that after she had heard the door open and shut once she came down, and saw the door half open—the prosecutor says he did not return for ten minutes, and he cannot say that in that time some one had not been into the room and taken the watch.
JOHN PRITCHARD . He did not go out with me—his brother was not at the house he sent me to.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

1022. JOHN PEARCE was indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 1st of March, 1 watch, value 3 l.; 1 watch chain, value 1 s.; and 2 watch keys, value 6 d.; the goods of Samuel Nicholls ' which had lately before been stolen by an evil-disposed person, he well knowing the same to have been stolen.
SAMUEL NICHOLAS . I am out of business. I was in Worship-square about half-past one o'clock in the day on the 1st of March, going home—I met a man who snatched out my watch, chain, and seals, and ran down Worship-street—I called "Stop thief," and some persons pursued, but I could not, as I am lame—I went to the pawnbrokers, and gave notice—I saw my watch the same evening.
JOHN BOARDS . I am a pawnbroker, and live at No. 42, Shoreditch—on the 1st of March, about ten minutes before four o'clock, the prisoner brought this watch to me to pledge—he asked 30 s. on it—I looked at it very hard, and said to my wife, "This is the watch I have received information about"—the prisoner said, "You have had it before for 30 s.—you can lend 30 s. on it"—I said, "How came you by it?"—he said it was his own, and he had given 3 l. for it, that he had had it six months, and pawned it several times for 30 s.—I came to the front to look for an officer, and gave him in charge.
Prisoner. He asked me to tell him the truth, where the watch came from—I said, "A young man, a sailor, stands at the door, who asked me to pawn it, and he said, "If they ask any questions, tell them they have had it before.' "
Witness. I did not ask him to tell the truth.
WILLIAM WEBB ( police-constable H 42.) I took the prisoner, and produce this watch—Mr. Boards said, in the hearing of the prisoner, that he had offered it in pledge; and he had received information of one that was stolen, and thought that was it—the prisoner said a man at the door gave it him, and said he would give him 1 s. to pledge it for him—there was a young man stood at the door when we came out—the prisoner looked at him. and said that he was not the man.
Prisoner's Defence. I was drawn into the foolishly—the young man appeared to me be a sailor—he was 16 or 17 years of age—I was on a sick bed for several days before—I have lived in that neighbourhood for twenty years.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY . Aged 37.— Transported for Seven Years.

1023. WILLIAM PRESWICK was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of March, 1 looking glass and stand, value 6 s., the goods of James Crisp; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
CHARLES M'INTOSH ( police-sergeant E 53.) I was in Queen's-row, Pimlico, on the 19th of March, at half-past seven o'clock in the evening—and saw the prisoner with something under his apron—I stopped him, and asked him what he had got—he said, a looking-glass, and a gentleman gave him a shilling to take it to the middle arch of Westminster-bridge—I asked him where the gentleman was—he said, "A little way back"—I asked him to go back—he went a little way, and then said he was gone—I took him to the station-house, and found Mr. Crisp, who lives about a mile and a-half from where I took the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Did you go with the prisoner to Westminster-bridge? A. No, Mr. Crisp lives in Lower Sloane-street—the prisoner said he had been paid for his trouble, and showed me the shilling.
JAMES CRISP . I am a broker, and live in Lower Sloane-street. This glass is mine—it was taken off a bedstead in my shop—it was there at four o'clock in the afternoon, when I went out—I returned late in the evening—it was then gone.
Cross-examined. Q. What time did you get home? A. About nine o'clock—I had not seen the glass for some hours.
WILLIAM DYKE ( police-constable R 193.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former indictment, which I got from the Clerk of the Peace
of Westminster—I was a witness on the trial—I know he is the man—( read.)
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Transported for Fourteen Years.

1025. MARY MURPHY was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 1 coat, value 10 s., the goods of Arthur James Lewis.
JOSEPH MILBANK . I live in Rawstorne-street, Clerkenwell, and am clerk to Arthur James Lewis—he has chambers there. On the 5th of April I heard a noise on the landing-place of the second floor, where I was sitting—I ran out, and saw the prisoner going down stairs—I overtook her on the first floor, and she threw this coat from her—it is my master a, and was taken from the room adjoining, the room I sat in—I gave her into custody.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Was that coat in your master's chambers? A. Yes—I saw it about ten minutes before—I did not see a boy on the staircase in a fustian jacket—the door was ajar—I cannot say whether the outer door was ajar—I did not hear any body else go down stairs—I saw the prisoner through the balustrades—she stopped on the first floor, and threw the coat from her—I suppose she saw me.
JURY Q. Did you lose sight of her? A. She got out and went to a public-house—she came out again, and was then taken, as the policeman was waiting for her at the door—I swear she is same woman.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not take the coat.
GUILTY . Aged 40.— Confined Six Months.

1026. RICHARD EWEN was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of March, 2 sacks, value 3 s., the goods of George Schunter.

1027. SOPHIA STUBBS was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 1 s.; 1 bag, value 2 d.; 2 sovereigns, 3 half-crowns. 8 shillings, 7 sixpences, and 4 d. in copper money. the goods and monies of Benjamin Clark, from his person.
BENJAMIN CLARK . I am a green-grocer. On the 5th of April I met the prisoner as near two o'clock as I can recollect—I was the worse for liquor, and hardly recollect any thing about the matter, but I know I had the money in my pocket when I met the prisoner in Bow-street—I went with her to a house, No 8, Charles-street, Drury-lane—I had then three sovereigns, and a little other money, but I cannot recollect what—I laid down on the bed—I had not given the prisoner any money—I was awoke about four o'clock by the mater of the house—the prisoner was then gone, and my money also—the master of the house had given me change for a sovereign.
THOMAS KEENE . I am landlord of the house. I remember the prisoner coming there, with the witness, about two o'clock on Tuesday morning—the prisoner had lodged there about six months—she took the prosecutor to her room, and as they were passing the passage, the prosecutor asked if I would give him change for a sovereign—I gave him three half-crowns, and the rest in small silver—I did not see that he had any other sovereigns—he then went up-stairs, and in the course of an hour after. wards (I had fastened the street door) I heard somebody coming down stairs—I opened my door, and saw the prisoner coming down, with her shoes in her hand—I asked where she was going—she said to get some gin—I said she should not go out till I saw that her friend was all right, knowing that he had had change of me—I then double-locked the door, went up-stairs, and tried to awake the prosecutor, but I could not—he was in bed, and I saw his clothes—he had no money left—I came down, and accused the prisoner, who was by the door, of robbing him of the change of a sovereign—she said he gave her 5 s., which was all the money she had—I sent for an officer, but previous to the officer coming, she said she did not want to rob him, and sooner than I should give her in charge, she would give me a bag containing 19 s. 4 d., and a silk handkerchief—the officer then came, and took hold of her throat, and two sovereigns came from her mouth into his hand.
PHILLIP COSTIN . I am a police-constable. On the morning of the 5th of April I was sent for between three and four o'clock—the prisoner was in the witness's room, and he gave me a bag containing 19 s. 4 d.—I saw there was something in the prisoner's throat—I seized her, and she gave me out two sovereigns.
BENJAMIN CLARK . This handkerchief if mine.
Prisoner's Defence. He gave me the money to take care off till the morning—it was not my intention to rob him, but so many persons coming in, I thought it was the best way to take it down with me—he had asked me to get something for supper, and to take the handkerchief to put it in—I delivered him (the landlord) all the silver and the handkerchief—I said, "I shall not let you have the two sovereigns"—I put them in my month.
GUILTY. of stealing, but not from the person. Aged 25.
Transported for Seven Years.

1029. GEORGE CARTER was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of April, 3 saddles, value 40 s.; 1 pair of traces, value 5 s.; 1 pair of hames, value 5 s.; 1 breeching, value 10 s.; 1 bridle, value 10 s.; 1 pair of reins, value 4 s.; 1 martingale, value 1 s.; and 1 horse-cloth, value 4 s.; the goods of Louis Panormo.
LOUIS PANORMO . I am a musical-instrument maker, and live in High-street, St. Giles. I lost this property from a stable which I rent in Falconbery-court—I had seen it safe in the morning of the 4th of April—I was sent for by Mr. Fox, at a quarter before nine o'clock in the evening—I found the padlock had been broken, and the stable was stripped—there were marks of an instrument by which it had been broken—this is my property.
WILLIAM FOX . I am a barman. At a quarter before nine o'clock last Monday evening I was in Falconberry-court, and saw a man coming out of the stable with a whip—the prisoner then came out with a bag of harness—I called to him and asked what he had got—he made no answer—I then collared him, and took him to the Three Tuns, where I reside—I sent for the prosecutor, who claimed the property.
SAMUEL GODDARD ( police-Sergeant F 13.) I took the prisoner and the property.
Prisoner's Defence. I was quite in distress—I really did not know what to do—I had been out of a situation for some time.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18360404-1030]
1030. MATILDA DE BOILET was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of March, 1 knife, value 2 s.; 1 fork, value 2 s.; 2 spoons, value 6 s.; 1 brooch, value 10 s.; 1 towel, value 6 d.; 1 petticoat, value 1 s.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 18 s.; and 5 yards of merino, value 10 s.; the goods of William Jenner.
WILLIAM JENNER . I live in Grove-street, Commercial-road. I have known the prisoner about two years—she lived in my house for six or seven weeks, like one of my family—on the 23rd of March, I and my wife went to a sale opposite Whitechapel church—I left the prisoner at home with a young man, who works in the room—I returned about five or six o'clock in the evening—the prisoner was then gone, and did not return at all—I missed a pair of trowsers from a box, and from the table drawer, which had been locked, and of which my wife had the key in her pocket, I missed the other articles.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How often have you allowed her to pawn things for you? A. Never—not once—when she came to me, she came as a binder, I am a shoemaker—I got her work, but she was discharged, as she did not to work enough—I work for Mr. Kirby, opposite Whitechapel church—I have no houses—I sold them at Lady-day—I had some in King-street, Commercial-road—that street is full of "accomodation houses"—I kept the houses as such, but I did not know what they were when I paid the deposit-money—I went to the Pavilion one night with my wife and the prisoner—the prisoner pressed me to send my wife home, and as I was rather fresh, the prisoner took me unawares—I do not know of any other time that she pressed me to leave my wife and family—I never had much to do with her since—I never went with her to one of my own houses.
GEORGE WALKER JUDGE . I am a police surveyor. I took the prisoner at Newington, and asked he what she had done with the things she took from Mr. Jenner—she said she had pawned them, and burnt the duplicates—I asked her if she knew any thing of a child's petticoat and
towel, pledged at Mr. Latter's—she first denied it, and then said she did—Mrs. Jenner gave me the duplicates, which she said she found under the prisoner's bed.
GEORGE GILL . I am a pawnbroker. I have two remnants of merino, pawned by a female—I cannot say who.
SARLE WHITLOW . I have a child's petticoat and a napkin, pawned by Elizabeth Adams—not by the prisoner.
GEORGE WORLEY . I have a knife, fork, two tea-spoons, and a brooch, pawned by a female—I cannot say whether it was the prisoner.
MARYAN JENNER . I am the prosecutor's wife. I found this duplicate under the bolster of the prisoner's best—this property is mine, and there was a great deal more, which we cannot find—I never gave her leave to pawn any thing.
Cross-examined. Q. When you had any thing to pledge, you did it yourself? A. Yes, always—I have pawned when we wanted money—the prisoner was sometimes out till nine or ten, or sometimes twelve o'clock at night—she has brought home money to me—I never asked her where she got it—my husband had five houses in King-street.
COURT. Q. Do not you know that the prisoner went with men? A. Yes—she came to us, and said she would get binding or umbrella-making—we always kept her—sometimes she gave me a shilling—the largest sum was 6 s., and part of that was my husband's money, that she had of him. NOT GUILTY .
OLD COURT, Monday, April 11, 1836.
Third Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

1032. JAMES HUTCHINSON was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of April, 2 brushes, value 5 s.; and 2 razors, value 4 s.; the goods of Charles John Parke.
JAMES CHRISTOPHER EVANS . I am a Thames police-officer. On the 4th of April, I met the prisoner in Hermitage-street, St. George's and apprehended him on another charge—I took him to the office, and between the waistband of his trowsers and his shirt, there were two hair brushes—I asked where he got them from—he said he bought them before he went to sea, and took them to Barbadoes for speculation—that he could not sell them, and was going to take them back—I asked where he bought them—he said of a Jew, but he could not find him—he said he belonged to the
Tropic —I went on board the vessel, and found the prosecutor—I searched the prisoner's chest, and found the razors in a pocket of a pair of trowsers in his box.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. I believe you took him for having tobacco which had not paid the duty? A. Yes; and he was fined 10 s.—the prosecutor said he did not wish to hurt him—he did not say they used to borrow each other's things.
CHARLES JOHN PARKE . I am a sailor on board the Tropic, which came from Barbadoes. The prisoner had belonged to that vessel—the had gone on shore on this day—these brushes and razors are mine, and were taken from my chest on board the ship—it was not locked.
Cross-examined. Q. How long had he sailed with you? A. This was the third voyage—we were good fiends—we did not borrow each other's things—he conducted himself well—I said I did not wish to hurt him, but I was obliged to prosecute him.
GUILTY. Aged 38.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Eight Days.

1034. JAMES DUNCAN was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of William Chaplin, on the 21st of March, at St. Andrew, Holborn, and stealing therein 16 half-crowns, 60 shilling, and 40 sixpences, the monies of James Maskell; and 8 sovereigns, 1 half sovereign, and 15 shillings, the monies of the said William Chaplin; and JAMES FALLEN for feloniously and maliciously inciting and procuring the said James Duncan to commit the said felony.
JOHN FLETCHER . I was waiting about at the White Horse, in Fetter-lane, for jobs, about half-past four o'clock on Tuesday morning, the 22nd of March, and I caught hold of a chap's stick who was in a coach—I said to him, "Is your name Hoppe?"—he said, "Yes"—I said, "You have no business here at al"—I knew both the prisoners by sight before that morning—I saw Duncan in the York coach—he had no business there—he was not employed there—I shut the door, and shut him in—he was alone—he said his mother had locked him out.
JAMES MASKELL . I am book-keeper at the White Horse. I left on Monday evening, the 21st of March, about a quarter before ten o'clock—the office is up the yard, and is part of the house, but there is no inlet to the office but from the yard—there is no communication with the dwelling-house, except by going through the yard—you must come out into the yard to go the house—there is simply a covered way from the office to the tavern, so that you can pass along without going into the rain—it is part of the house—the rooms over the officer are bed-rooms for gentlemen stopping at the tavern—when I went away at night, I left my desk double locked, and a bar of iron went inside the lid of the desk through the bottom of the desk, where there is a plate of iron which fastens with a padlock—I left the porter in the office when I left—his name is Greenland—he is not here—there was £6 silver, loose in the desk—there were two parcels also which seemed to contain money, which had come by the coach that evening, and which I placed in the desk.
for security—I do not know myself what they contained—there was also some silver in a bag which was not taken, as something last over it—I was sent for between six and seven o'clock next morning by the porter, and found the desk had evidently been forced open—the padlock had been forced, the tongue of it wrenched up so as to allow the how of the padlock to pass it, and the brass work of the desk-lock had been forced—I missed those parcels and the loose silver—I examined the office-door—that had not been forced—I made inquiry, and on questioning Fletcher, in consequence of what he said, I went in quest of Duncan, and gave him into custody—I found him in the passage of his mother's lodging—the officer searched him, in my presence, and in his boot found four shillings, two of them were new—he told me something at the station-house—I made him no threat or promise—it was entirely voluntary—the officer told me he was crying—when I entered the station-house he was crying, and as soon as he saw me, he said, "I did it, Mr. Maskell, and will tell you all about it"—he said he got into the York coach, which was brought from the coach-factory, and stood in the lane—that he got inside the coach, and remained there till it was drawn into the yard again, at the gates being closed, and at one o'clock he got out, and let himself into my office with a key that he had got—that he took a poker and forced the padlock, and then forced the desk and took the two parcels and the money—that he had been instigated to do it by another boy—he said that he had got a light with some matches and a candle, and that when he had done it, he got out and got into the York coach, where Fletcher saw him, and remained there till the gates were opened, and was drawn in the coach into Fetter-lane—that he then got out, and went to the house of another boy not in custody, and opened the parcel—took out the money and placed it in a cream-jug, to remain there till the matter had blown over—that he took five shillings and spent one, and the remaining four shillings, were in his boot—he said one parcel contained sovereigns, half-sovereigns, and five shillings, which he afterwards corrected, and side was seven shillings—and the other parcel contained a good deal of money—he did not know how much—that they placed all the money in a cream-jug, to save it till it had blown over—he formerly had something to do at our yard—the principal park of the loose silver in my desk was new money, and two of the four shilling found on him were new.
JOSEPH SHAW . I am a policeman. I received Duncan in charge from Maskell—I searched him, and found four shillings in the boot of his left leg—I was present when he mentioned the particulars to Maskell—I have heard what he has stated, it is correct—he told me the same thing before, but not exactly in the same words—I made him no promise or threat—I went to Plough-court, and, while I was watching, Fallen came down stairs—there was another officer there, and he gave him into custody—Fallen told me that he stole the key of the office and gave it to Duncan, on the Saturday morning—this was after his first examination, as I was taking him from the justice-room to the Compter—I had not promised or threatened him in any way.
CHARLES WALLER . I am a city policeman. I found a key at a house in Wych-street, Strand, behind a water-butt, where Hagger lived, who is not apprehended—I showed the key to Duncanhe said it was the key which he took to Hagger's house with the two parcels of the money—I took it to the office, and it fitted the door.
JAMES MASKELL re-examined. The covered passage leads from the office
door to the tavern door—it is brought forwards from the building to the coaches, that passengers may alight without being in the rain—the covered way is not enclosed—one side is the wall of the house, and the other side is exposed to the yard—it is supported by posts, but not enclosed—there is no door communicating from the office into the house—you must go under the passage way, under cover.
Duncan. I broke the desk open—I was forced by Hagger, and brought into a bad line to do it.
Fallen. This boy asked me to get the key—I did not know what he was going to do.
Duncan. Yes he did.
DUNCAN — GUILTY of Larceny only. Aged 15.
Transported for Seven Years.
FALLEN— NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Justice Gaselee.
[trialAccount t18360404-1035]
1035. JAMES BARNES was indicted for embezzling and stealing, on the 2nd of March, the sum of 6 s. 4 d., 4 d., and 2 s. 6 d., which he had received by virtue of his employment in his Majesty's Post Office.—11 other COUNTS, varying the manner of laying the charge.
MESSRS. SHEPHERD, ADOLPHUS, and SCARLETT conducted the prosecution.
WASTELL ARROWSMITH . I am a clerk in the Foreign Office of the Post Office; the prisoner was one of the Presidents of that office, and had the control over the inferior officers. I received instructions respecting the prisoner, which made my attention to him more exact, and my memory more accurate—letters received at the receiving-houses, in different parts of the town, for inland and foreign delivery, are made up in bags for the evening, to he delivered at the Post Office—it often happens that Colonial letters are put into the foreign instead of the inland bags—( the evidence given by the witness in the former case, page 1016, as to the routine of business at the Post Office, was here read to him, which he confirmed.)—when letters come by mistake into the Foreign Office, they are sent to the Inland Office, by a tunnel, with the money—the money ought not to come into the hands of the President—he ought to send the letters by a messenger to the bag-clerk—that is the usual course of the office—the prisoner had been in the habit of coming himself, instead of sending a messenger, to get the money—I had been spoken to to pay particular attention to Mr. Barnes—on Wednesday evening, the 2nd of March, I was on duty as window-clerk—I came on duty between eight and nine o'clock in the morning, and remained on duty all day till half-past seven—the prisoner came to me, about a quarter before five o'clock, with three letters, which he said were to go
into the Inland Office, and it required 6 s. 4 d. as the postage—he also read out the amounts of the postage of each letter—one amounted to 1 s. 2 d.—I cannot say where it was for, but it was very peculiarly marked—I am swear to the appearance of the letter—the others were 2 s. 2 d. and 3 s. (looking at a letter)—I believe this is the 1 s. 2 d. letter, as far as I can state—it has a penny-post mark on it, which is another thing which made me notice it—it was a busy might at the office; but at the time he brought me the letters I was not doing any thing at all—I gave him 6 s. 4 d.—he came about five minutes after, and brought two newspapers, which he said were going to the Mauritius and Madras—he got 4 d. for them—he came again be-seven and eight o'clock, and asked me for 2 s. 2 d. for a letter which was to go into the Inland Office—I think he mentioned that it was to go to Nova Soctia✗—I told him, as I had not a letter agreeing with the amount 2 s. 2 d., he must go to the examiner of the bags, Mr. James—a Nova Scotia letter would property go to the inland office—I was present on Saturday, the 5th of March, at Mr. Peacock's office, when the prisoner was examined—Mr. Peacock came down to the Foreign Office, and he went up with him—Mr. Peacock asked him whether he had forwarded letters which he had been in the habit of coming for the postage for to the Inland Office—he said, "Yes, of course"—he was asked if he had any letters in his drawer—he said, "No"—he was asked if he had any about his person—he said, "No"—when he was told he would be searched, he acknowledged that he had some in his drawer—he was told by the solicitor it would be necessary to hare his person and drawer searched; and he said, a short time afterwards, "Now you ask me particularly, I recollect I have four or five letters in my drawer'—he never said he had any about him that I recollect—two foreign letters were found on his person.
Cross-examined by MR. PRICE. Q. How long have you been a clerk in the Post Office? A. About three years and a half—I am very conversant with the general routine of the Post Office duties—I expect to rise. as we are in the habit of doing, as vacancies occur—when a President is removed, I come rather near the presidency—Mr. Barnes's situation produced him about 500 l. a year, I rather think—he has always been considered a very able, intelligent gentleman, and an excellent officer, knowing the duties of the Post Office very well—but not performing them.
Q. Do you mean to tell us this gentleman was not active, able officer who performed his duties well? A. Yes, I do—I have been frequently with him in the office—it was his business to superintend my conduct—it does not occur to me not to make mistakes—the duties are not extremely intricate—it requires no skill or experience at all to make up bundles of letters—it requires to he done with great expedition on busy days—the business ought all to be completed by twelve o'clock—Mr. Barnes has assisted the junior clerks on busy nights—in doing so, he has undoubtedly done more than his own personal duty—it is the usual way to sent the money through the hands of a messenger with the letters to go to the Inland Office—it is the usual and proper way with a president—when Mr. Barnes did it, it would alleviate the duty of the messenger—Mr. Barnes would be entitled to transfer the money he receives for the postage through the tunnel by the hand of a messenger—he was not compelled to carry them to the tunnel himself—if I had been disengaged I would have done it, if he had asked me—we are all together in the office, but not promiscously—the messengers are on one side, and the clerks on the other—there are fire places in the office, round one of which we(the clerks) gather, and the messengers round the other—the President and clerks mix round the fire—the messengers are in the habit of stirring our fire—the messengers are not allowed to come and sit with us—they come when we send for them—I do not know whether the President gives security to the Crown for the situation he holds—I am in the habit of attending the window, but I am not called the window clerk—it I a term used among us—there are seven of us altogether—Mr. Barnes may have sent the money to the tunnel by any of the inferior clerks—probably if he had asked a President he would not have objected; hut there being only one President on duty, he could not ask him—there are two Presidents, and a superintending President in the office—there are sixteen clerks.
Q. Now you say Mr. Barnes was in the habit of coming to you for these letters, did you ever refuse him on any occasion? A. Never—the usual course is to send a messenger—I could not object to Mr. Barnes having the money and letters from me—he might think proper to send the messenger to the tunnel—my business finished about thirty five minutes after seven o'clock, on the 2nd of March—I think Mr. Barnes finished about eight o'clock—I can say it is before twelve o'clock—his duty would finish at eleven o'clock—that was the time of their leaving every night, except post nights—that is the time they are authorized to leave—on Tuesdays sad Fridays they are sometimes there till after twelve o'clock—he is not obliged to sleep at the Post Office on Tuesdays and Fridays—I cannot tell whether he slept there on the Wednesday night—there is a bed room and sitting room on purpose for the President—I believe Mr. Barnes has a wife and family, and a house in the country—I know he has been in the habit of sleeping at the Post Office on busy night.
COURT, Q. What time do you go away? A. On Tuesdays and Fridays at twelve o'clock—on other nights it varies—I always sleep there myself.
MR. PRICE. Q. When Mr. Barnes came to you for the letters, he came at a time when you were particularly at leisure? A. Yes, when I had nothing to do at all—I was not employed on any thing at the time—my attention was not then drawn by any important office business—that did not give me an opportunity of examining the letters particularly—I could have done it—he came to me the second time about five minutes after—he might have come at a time when I was more fully employed; and he did so, between seven and eight o'clock—he was first asked in the Solicitor's Office whether he had forwarded the letters he had been in the habit of receiving the postage on—I was asked at Bow street what he said at Mr. Peacock's office—I do not think I made the same statement on that occasion—I answered the questions that were asked me.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. You have been asked about the views you have in this prosecution; does it make any difference to you in your he pas or your situation, whether the prisoner is convicted or acquitted? A. None whatever—he is already removed from the office, and a successor appointed—whatever may happen to day, I shall be no more backward of forward in my appointment.
MR. PRICE. Q. Was Mr. Barnes asked about money?—have you told us all that was said to him in the solicitor's office—have you told us all that passed? A. He was asked if he forwarded the letters on Friday with the money—there are colonial letters almost daily—letters for Madras and Mauritius, and other colonial places, frequentle come to the Foreign Office.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Have the mistakes, which you say have occurred, occurred when Mr. Barnes was President, or when any body else was President? A. Both—no mistake was ever made by me which subjected me to any police inquiry—there is no great nicety required in conducting letters through the tunnel—there is a person appointed to do it—if any body else chose to do it, he has nothing to do but walk to the tunnel and put it in—the messenger merely comes to our fire place when called, and leaves again—the number of window clerks on duty at the same time varies—there was none besides myself on duty on the 2nd of March—I was the only window clerk that night—Mr. Barnes did not order me to take the letter and money to the tunnel—I did not hear anybody ordered to do so—the tunnel closed that evening at eight o'clock—he had from
five to eight o'clock to send the letters through the tunnel—i said nothing to him about receiving letters out of the usual course, because I had no business to interfere with a superior officer.
COURT. Q. Suppose the President had sent the messenger to the bag-man, and the bagman had received the money and the letter, what would be the messenger's duty? A. He would take it, with the money, to the tunnel, and put it into the box to be conveyed over—that is the usual course—he would leave the foreign letter with Mr. Barnes, and take the money and inland letters to the tunnel—it would be the messenger's duty to take the money and the inland letter to the tunnel—he might or might not have to carry the foreign letter, it might be handed to him by the bagman himself—he might take the foreign letter to the President.
MR. SCARLETT. Q. When foreign letters are brought into the office, ought they not, in the common course of duty to be forwarded the same night? A. They ought to be forwarded the same night.
FREDERICK DIXON JAMES . I am a clerk in the Foreign Post Office. On the 2nd of March, between seven and half-past seven o'clock, the prisoner came to me to ask for the postage of one letter and two newspapers—the postage of the letter was 2 s.2 d.—I have a distinct recollection of that—the newspapers were 2 d. each—I delivered him the money—I cannot my whether it is the usual course of duty for the President or messenger to bring the letters to receive the postage for—the other President receives them by a messenger—the letters on which I paid the postage were intended for the Inland Office—I remember placing a letter on the table behind me for the messenger to take to the Inland Office—it was addressed to the name of Teasdale—I do not recollect whether it was Mr. or Mrs.—I sent 8 d., the postage, with the letter—there is an officer of the name of Teasdale in the Foreign Office—I do not know whether the letter was written by him—it is usual for clerks in the Foreign Office to transmit their letters for the Inland Office through the tunnel (looking at a letter)—the postage on this letter is 2 d.,—it is addressed to "W. L. Mellish, Esq., Rifle Brigade, Halifax, Nova Scotia"—it bears the Foreign Office stamp,—according to the stamp, it came into the office on the 2nd of March—this newspaper ( looking at one) is addressed to "A. M'Lean, Esq., Civil Service Madras"—it bears the stamp of the Foreign Office, according to which, it came into the Foreign Office on the 29th of February—the postage of that is 2 d.—this letter addressed to "Hev. Ham, Curacoa, "bears the Foreign Office stamp—that came into the Foreign Office on the 29th of February—and this, addressed to "New South Wales," came on the 20th of February—letters coming to the Foreign Office ought to be forwarded the same night—I am acquainted with these stamps—I have examined the letters, with the marks and signs used by the Post Office, to ascertain that they came into the office on those dates.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How long have you been a clerk in the Post Office? A. Four years—there are some nights in the Post Office much more busy than others—I do not recollect whether the 2nd of March was a heavy night or not—it might have been very heavy that night—( the night-charge book was here produced.)
Q. Look at this book, can you tell me whether there was not made up on that night the Malta, the Indian Brazil, and Buenos Ayres monthly mails, in addition to the other business? A. Some part of them were made up such letters as were not dispatched on the previous Saturday—I mean letters which came in after the Saturday, as the mails were detained—that
would render the Wednesday night rather more busy than ordinary—I did not consider I was doing wrong in leaving the letter and postage on the table for the messenger to take—the president might, in the discharge of his duty, leave a letter and the postage on a table for the messenger to take)—I cannot say how long my letter remained on the table before the messenger took it—I did not see it taken—I cannot tell what messenger took it—it would have been the duty of any messenger, passing that way, to take it, and put it into the tunnel—if Mr. Barnes had asked any of the clerks to put it into the tunnel, they would of course done so—there are sixteen clerks in the office, including the Presidents, and four messengers—I did not have the letters in my hand to read when Mr. Barnes spoke to me—I have not the slightest idea whether these are the letters or newspapers.
MR. SCARLETT. Q. You have been asked about a press of business on this particular night—if there was, would it not save the time of the President to employ a messenger, instead of going himself to demand the postage? A. Yes, if the messenger was not engaged at the time.
COURT. Q. You say it would be the duty of any messenger to take this letter—do you mean any messenger who happened to be going that way? A. The messengers are continually coming in to carry the empty bags, and we always send letters by them to the tunnel—if he sees a letter lying there, he might take it and the money, without being directed—I do not know that this is his duty—we usually direct them—I called for a messenger to take the letter which I put behind me, with the postage—we do not allow a messenger to take a letter from behind us without being directed to do so—it was Mr. Teasdale's letter that I put behind me—no letter coming to the Post Office in the usual way could arrive at the Inland Office through the Foreign Office, without the directions of the President or myself, as the opener of the bag—a letter written in the office would go by my directions, by putting the postage on the table—the President might have sent either of these letters in any way he thought fit.
MR. PRICE. Q. Does not the maker up of the bundle occasionally send a messenger for the money, without the knowledge of the President? A. I never heard of such a circumstance.
RICHARD CRADDOCK . On Wednesday evening, the 2nd of March, I was on duty at the tunnel, on the Inland Office—it is my duty to receive the letters and postage, which come from the Foreign Office to the Inland Office by the tunnel—I went on duty at a quarter before five o'clock, and left at eight o'clock—I was induced to pay particular attention that evening to the letters and postage which came through—I received one private letter, belonging to a clerk in the office, with the postage of 8 d.—it was addressed to Teasdale, of Bognor, and I think Mrs. Teasdale—there is a clerk in the Foreign Post Office, of the name of Teasdale—it is the custom of the clerks to transmit their letters in that way—I receive them frequently in that way—I received two newspapers on that evening for the Ship-letter Office—the postage was 2 d —I did not notice their address, more than that they were for the Ship-letter Office—I did not receive any postage of 6 s.4 s., nor of 4 d. that evening—I received no other postage than what I have stated—the tunnel closes at eight o'clock—I remained there till the tunnel closed.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. At which end is the tunnel closed? A. The inland end—I work the boxes by machinery—there are two boxes—one goes to the Foreign Office, while the other box comes from it—when
I close my door there is still a box at the foreign end—I do not knows when I close my door whether the door at the foreign end is open on closed—if any thing was thrown into the box at the foreign end after I left, it must remain there till the following morning—they do not work the tunnel at the other end—I am in attendance every day—I was not on duty the following morning—mistakes do occur, which are rectified in the course of the evening—I never knew it happen that a letter was found in the box the next morning—the tunnel works from six to eight o'clock, which is the time when the Post Office is most engaged—there had been short postage sent with letters by mistake, and we have returned an answer to the Foreign Office, that they have sent the postage so much short, and they have sent the money to make it up—I do not know whether there is any officer superintending at the foreign end of the tunnel to take an account of what goes through—I believe the letter and money are put in by different officers at the other end, but at out end there is only one—I keep no account of what comes through the tunnel—I take the money and letter to that part of the office they are to go to—some to the paid window, others to the Ship-letter Office—there is no check kept on my accuracy to my knowledge—there is no ticket or note of the money or letters accompanying them—the Ship-letter Office is one one side of my room, and the paid window on the other—if a colonial letter comes through the tunnel from the Foreign Office, I take it to the window, and state, "This is from the Foreign Office, and there is the postage"—Bullbeck, the other messenger, was on duty following morning.
MR. SHEPHERD. Q. You receive the letters and money through the tunnel and them to their proper places, and account to the person you take them to, how you obtain them? A. Yes—the runnel is worked on my side—they can only work it on the foreign side by pulling a cord, which is not the usual way—we have a bell on each side—if they want to send any thing to me they ring the bell, and if I want to send to them I do the same.
CHARLES BULLBECK . I am a messenger in the Post Office. It is my duty at attend at the tunnel on alternate days—I was there on Thursday, the 3rd of March—I went at six o'clock in the morning—there was no letters or postage in the box that morning, I am quite certain.
Cross-examined by MR. PRICE. Q. How long do you remain on duty? A. Till about nine o'clock in the morning on the average—it depends on when the duty ceases—i am there about hours—when we leave the duty is over till evening entirely.
GEORGE LEADBITTER . I am a police-officer. I was present at the Post Office when the prisoner was examined by Mr. Peacock—Mr. Peacock asked him if he had any letters in his drawer—(that was the first question I heard; I was not present at first)—he said he would tell the truth—I had not gone into the room till some after the prisoner was there—I found these letter in his drawer—( producing the same letter as on the former trial.)
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. I believe these papers and letters you produced on Saturday also? A. I did—I found the keys of the drawer on his person—he pointed out key which would open his drawer—the first thing I heard him say was, "I will tell the truth"—I did not use the words on Saturday, but the meaning was the same.
Q. Did you use the words on Saturday, as coming from him, "I will tell the truth?" A. No—I said on Saturday, that the said he had
letters and papers in his drawer—it did not occur at me to mention that he said he would tell the truth—I was examine at Bow-street—I did not mention it there—it occurred to me after my examination on Saturday—I examined Mr. Barnes' house—he had not any opportunity of going to it, before searched it—I found an official book and some Post Office official papers—I searched his private apartments at the office, and found a quantity of papers.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Where is the house you searched? A. At Peckham, Rye, about six miles from the Office.
WASTELL ARROWSMITH re-examined by MR. PRICE. Q. Is there not a certain sum of the public money which the official clerks in the office have access to and may use? A. For official purpose—I cannot tell whethers there is any authority for using it—it has been a practice ever since I was in the office—it is necessary—the money is entrusted to the junior—he is answerable for it to the post-master—it is allowed to him for official parposes, by the superintending President—it is 40 l., I believe at present—it used to be 35 l. when I was junior—it is used for the purpose of returning over-charges on letter and other official purpose—there are letter charged to him, coming by the mails from France and other places—he is to pay the money with which he is changed on these mornings, on a certain day—three days in a week, Monday, Wednesday, and Friday.
Q. Is any part of that money lent to the junior clerks? A. It has been, though that is at his own risk—he is answerable for it—I have myself borrowed some of that money, for my own purpose—it is borrowed by the senior as well as by the junior—Mr. Barnes himself has borrowed it.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. How often does the junior clerk account for the money in his possession? A. How often does the junior clerk accounted for once a week—it is given by one junior to his successor—we get the return of the overcharges once a week, and always keep up the sum—that been nothings at all to do with called upon through the tunnel.
(The prisoner being called upon for his defence, contended that by the 5th and 6th William IV, caput 25, sects. 8, 17, 28, and 24, he was authorised to exercise a discretionary power in the mode of transmitting letters—that on the night in question several addition mails required to be made up, which caused an immense pressure of business, and induced him to render his assistance in those inferior departments which his duty not require—that on retiring to bed, after every lock in the office was secured, he observed that two single letters were left, out, which were of no consequences, there being no postage on them, they being directed for ordinary seaman—he placed them in his pocket, for security, till the morning—that if the money, stated by the witnesses as paid to him, ever came into his hands, he must have forwarded it with letters to the Inland-office—he further stated, that the junior clerks had an interest in his removal, as they would thereby be advanced—and contended that it must be evident he should never have had the folly or madness to risk his situation of 500 l. per annum, after twenty-seven years service, as well as his character and the interests of his wife and family, for so paltry a sum. The same witnesses deposed to his good character as on the former trial.)
NOT GUILTY .
Fourth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

On the 7th of April, about ten o'clock in the day, the prisoner passed my window, and put his hand on the shopboard with his apron own it—a person said he bad taken a bit of bacon—I ran after him, and caught him with it in his hand, four or five yards off—I gave him in charge.
Prisoner's Defence. A boy came running by me, and threw the bacon into the road, and ran off—Mr. Russell laid hold of me—a gentleman said, "It is not him, the boy is gone on"—he immediately said he would make me pay for it.
MR. RUSSELL. It is not tune—he had the bacon is his hand when I collared him, and he threw it the road.

1041. MARY ANN BROOKS was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of April, 1 shirt value 2 s.; 2 night caps, value 1 s.; 8 handkerchiefs, value 12 s.; 1 ring, value 2 s.; 27 plates, value 6 s.; 2 books value 1 s.; 1 brush, value 1 s.; 8 stocking, value 3 s.; 3 dishes, value 1 s.; 1 pin cushion, value 1 s.; 1 rule value 1 s.; 2 night gowns, value 6 s.; 1 towel, value 1 s.; and 1 pillow-case, value 2 s.; the goods of Elizabeth Richardson, her mistress.
ELIZABETH RICHARDSON . I live I Cochrane-tenace, St. John's-wood.
The prisoner has been in my service for twelve months as servant of all work—I intended to part with her—I received a letter which raised my suspicions—I missed articles, and found she was carrying things out of my house—I stopped her last Tuesday going out, as she said, to her sister's funeral—she had a cloak on—I saw it was very bulky—I said, "Come here, take off your cloak, and let me see what you have in your pocket"—she was then in the front parlour—she had her bonnet on going out—when I spoke to her, she ran into the bed-room, threw the cloak on the bed, and began taking things out of her pocket—she took three shirts out—she kicked two under the bed, and I took hold of the other, but I took out of her pocket some of the articles named in the indictment—I sent for a policeman, who took her to the station-house—she had put a small ear-ring in a pillbox under a pillow at the time—she was let go for two days to go to her sister's funeral, and when she came back, the magistrate said I had better have her box searched—I found she had packed it full of china out of a crate—every thing in her box was mine—I was moving from a house I have, and had my china in a crate.
WILLIAM LLOYD . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody—I never saw the shirts—I was sent to the prosecutrix's house—the prisoner had been stopped, and her mistress gave me a night-shirt and other articles, and a small ring, which I found on the table—I took her before the Magistrate, and from the prosecutrix's kindness she was allowed to go to her sister's funeral, and her mistress engaged to produce her on the Friday morning—when she came before the Magistrate, he asked if I had searched her boxes—I then went to the house, searched her boxes, and found a number of plates, clothes, brushes, and other articles—here are some plates and dishes—the things were in five boxes.
Prisoner. Q. Where did you find all these different things? A. In her different boxes—I found the apparel in a box by itself.
ELIZABETH RICHARDSON re-examined. This crockery and these other things are all mine.
Prisoner's Defence. Her son-in-law gave me the stockings when we lived in York-terrace—the prosecutrix removed three times—she always allowed me to put the things into my box when we moved, as she had not many boxes—when we moved once the plate was put into the plate-box, and the china was put into my box, as the crate would not hold it—she told me whatever I might not be able to put into the crate, to put into my box, as we could get them out when wanted—she left the care of the linen to me—on Saturday I had a letter from my brother, saying my sister was ill, and wanting me to leave—on Tuesday, master was shaving, and said, "Bring me a clean shirt before you go," and mistress said, "Put out a clean pair of sheets"—I took them out, ready for the bed—my mistress said, "Do not stop longer"—I put the clean shirt in my pocket, and forgot to give it to master, as his door was locked; and as I was going out, she said, "Let me see what you have in your pocket?"—I had swept the ring off the carpet just before we moved—she allowed me to go my sister's funeral—she was angry at my leaving, and said before my face that she would prosecute me, if possible, having told the Magistrate, on Tuesday, she would not.
MRS. RICHARDSON re-examined. I do not know whether she attended her sister's funeral after I found the things—every word she has said is false—I did not know she had robbed me till the Tuesday—she was taken up about four months ago—she had robbed me then to a very large amount—I
did not intend to keep her—I did not suspect her—she laid things about the garden, and said somebody had got into the house—she went and called three policemen in to search the house—she took such dreadful oaths of her innocence, that I thought she could not guilty, and yet I did not intend to keep her, as I thought she could not be trustworthy—when before the Magistrate she owned that she stole the things—I did not know that I could charge her with stealing the sheets, for I did not take them out of her pocket.
JURY. Q. In the course of moving from one house to the other, were you aware that the prisoner had part of the linen in her box? A. No—on my oath, she had not—she got a false key, and took them out while I was out.
WILLIAM LLOYD re-examined. The prisoner was liberated to attend her sister's funeral, for two days, and afterwards returned—the boxes were locked—I had to force them all—the prisoner was not present when I opened them—I never spoke to her about the dishes. NOT GUILTY .

1042. JOSEPH TIMBRELL, JOHN MALONEY , and JOHN MARTIN , were indicted for stealing, on the 4th of April, 1 pair of trowsers, value 4 s. 6 d., the goods of Edward Medlicot Thomas.

JAMES THOMAS . I am brother to Edward Medlicot Thomas, a hosier and glover, at the corner of the Haymarket. These trousers belong to him—they hung inside the door on a nail—I saw them about seven o'clock—I did not see either of the prisoners about.
Timbrell. They hung outside the door.
Martin's Defence.—As I was going towards the square these boys had the trowsers—I asked what they had—they said a pair of trowsers which they had found in Golden-square—I was just going to look at them, when the officer came and took us all three into custody.
TIMBRELL— GUILTY . Aged 17.

1044. DAVID EVANS was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of April, 1 box, value 1 s., 3 lbs. of copper tubing, value 2 s., the goods of George Frederick Eckstein, another his masters.
GEORGE FREDERICK ECKSTEIN .—I am an ironmonger, and live in High Holborn. The prisoner has been about four years in my employ as bell-hanger—he was taken up on account of my property being found on him.
HENRY GILES . I am a policeman. I stopped the prisoner in Holborn, nearly opposite his master's shop. with this property in his tool-bag which I produce—his master desired me to stop him—he was going out with a job.
GEORGE THORNTON . I am a policeman. I searched the prisoner's lodging—he told me it was his lodging—I there found this tin box under the bed, which his master claims.
GEORGE FREDERICK ECKSTEIN re-examined I gave the prisoner in charge on Saturday, about quarter to one o'clock—i searched his lodging afterwards—I got my brother to mark this copper on the premises, and about two hours after it was missing—i then ran after the prisoner and had him stopped, and it was found in his bag—it is our property—it is worth 2 s.—he bore a very good character, and has been with me seven or eight years.
Prisoner's Defence. I am in the habit of working in the shop, where there are seven or eight men—sometimes I am sent out without my bag, and sometimes with it—when I was stopped by the policeman, I was not aware there was any copper in the bag—I have no doubt some of the men put it there—I think some of them had some spite against me.
Jury to MR. ECKSTEIN. Q. Have all your men bags alike? A. No; some of them have—no man put the copper into the prisoner's bag—I had information, and sent him out on a job, and went with my brother and marked the copper in my box—he had his bag out with him then—when he returned he was sent out again, and then he put it into bag—there was no spite on the part of his shopmates.
GUILTY. Aged 34— Recommended to mercy — Confined There Months.
